February 10, 2024 12:58 AM GMT

# Enter the Dragon

The Year of the Dragon may bring something other than longevity to the economic expansion and prosperity to financial markets brought by the Year of the Rabbit. A year with less liquidity provision and fiscal support raises downside risks, which should keep safe havens dear. Stay long duration.

## Global Macro Strategy

We discuss the underappreciated fiscal and liquidity backdrop to US economic resilience in 2023, and how that backdrop should change this year. We also discuss how changes in long-end Treasury supply appear to have little bearing on auction tails, but matter for the USD.

## Interest Rate Strategy

We stay long duration in the US via long 5y USTs. We close pay Apr ECB OIS meeting and enter Jun 24 1y midcurve 97.875/98.250 strangle; we close 10s30s OAT ASW Box and enter RX/UB invoice spread steepener box; we enter a receive position on EUR 5y5y (vs 6m) and maintain EUR 5y5 vs 20y5y steepener. In cash, we keep our structural short 10y BTP versus Bund. We maintain receive May'24 MPC and long UKT 1T37 vs. short UKT 1T57, and we also keep our short 15y ASW. We maintain receiving 2y OIS, short 30y JGB ASW. We enter long 20y JGBs on 10s20s30s fly.

## Currency & Foreign Exchange

We think it's too soon to turn USD bullish, but risks are skewed to the upside. GBP could gain from the UK March budget should it amplify its carry/ vol ratio further. We shift from short GBP/NOK to short CAD/NOK positions. We analyze whether Treasury auctions matter for USD and find that the USD is particularly sensitive to yield moves driven by 10y and 30y auctions. Stay long NOK/SEK given rising risk of aggressive cuts from the Riksbank. We see limited risk that USD/JPY will go higher from current levels.

## Inflation-Linked Bonds

We examine the strong performance of breakevens this week, and highlight why a cooling inflation profile keeps us away from being long. We stay neutral on breakevens.

## Short-Duration Strategy

We maintain short 2y swap spreads, review MMF holdings, and discuss recent AUM/WAM trends.

## Interest Rate Derivatives

Forwards have been pricing in flat swap curves for some time, yet history suggests curves will steepen much more than what is currently priced. We explain why we prefer long 5y UST to capture this view over conditional bull steepeners.

# Global Macro Strategy

## A Fire-Breathing Year of the Dragon

For those born in 1976, 1988, or 2000; the Year of the Dragon in 2024 should resonate. The Year of the Dragon may bring something other than longevity to the economic expansion and prosperity to financial markets brought by the Year of the Rabbit in 2023.

In contrast to the European dragon, the Chinese dragon doesn't breathe destructive fire. Nevertheless, we see risks to economic activity in 2024 that look like smoke. If there is a fire after all, central bank liquidity might not be ready to put it out. For now, though, investors are focused on a different type of heat: that coming from a hot US economy. Three ideas dominate investor thinking today:

1. Monetary policy isn't as restrictive as policy makers believe, as evidenced by above-trend real GDP growth in 2023 and to start 2024.

2. Monetary policy affects economic activity primarily through evolving financial conditions, which have eased more recently.

3. If monetary policy responds to lower inflation exclusively, financial conditions will ease further – sowing the seeds of an inflation rebound.

The latter two ideas overplay the causal relationship between financial conditions and economic activity. Yes, the level of financial conditions and changes in activity appear related (see Exhibit 1 ). But, statistical analysis of changes in financial conditions and changes in activity fall far short of providing evidence of a robust, causal relationship.

Exhibit 1: GS financial conditions index vs. ISM composite purchasing managers index
![](images/a115cfc2902b7030f9c6eda5126ab30610ad569c4cdd3a1ab4e2324a6a146bc7.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg

Exhibit 2: GS financial conditions index vs. ISM composite purchasing managers index
![](images/2a97ab0a8e08972e231b529afaf8fd17863a24e06ff67f359665a6711fa68034.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg

Even the visually appealing relationship between levels of financial conditions and changes in activity looks spurious – at least when it comes to soft economic data like purchasing manager indexes (PMIs). Indeed, the causality appears to shift over time.

Sometimes, a set of financial conditions appear to influence changes in activity. Other times, changes in activity generate a certain set of financial conditions, e.g., economic data move markets and influence central bank policies, which may subsequently affect financial conditions (see Exhibit 2 ).

FOMC participants don't seem as concerned with financial conditions as investors do, but share worries about higher inflation reemerging anyway. We wonder whether investors and FOMC participants may be missing the forest for the trees, given the potential deflationary impact from China coming from over-investment and over-capacity.

What about the idea that monetary policy isn't very restrictive? Growth data suggest that the US economy handled higher interest rates better in 2H23 than in 1H23 – hinting at a new, higher regime for equilibrium real rates. Perhaps the neutral rate returned to its pre-Great Financial Crisis (GFC) norms after all?

Count us skeptical. Rather, we think a combination of extraordinary fiscal stimulus and meaningful liquidity injection prevented monetary policy tightness from showing clearly in data and financial market prices.

• Risk-free rates compete against riskier asset classes for capital. As risk-free rates rise, reflecting tighter monetary policy, they usually drain broad liquidity, i.e., tighten financial conditions.

• However, fiscal policy and more narrow liquidity blunted the effect of higher riskfree rates in 2023, we think (see Do Deficits Destroy Liquidity? for more on narrow vs. broad liquidity).

Exhibit 3 and Exhibit 4 contextualize the fiscal firepower and narrow liquidity deluge in 2023 by comparing it to the preceding 5 years:

Exhibit 3: US Treasury financing need calculated from the Daily Treasury Statement\*

Exhibit 4: Changes in reserve balances (narrow liquidity) at the Federal Reserve

Source: Morgan Stanley, Federal Reserve, Bloomberg

• The US Treasury's financing need – what we call the "cash flow deficit" of the federal government – totaled \$2.111 trillion in 2023 (+7.6pp of nominal GDP) vs. \$1.309 trillion in 2022 (+5.0pp), equating to a 52% Y/Y increase.

• Reserve balances held at the Federal Reserve increased \$0.44 trillion in 2023 (+1.6pp of nominal GDP) vs. decreased \$1.03 trillion in 2022 (-3.9pp), equating to a 5.5pp of nominal GDP swing in liquidity provision Y/Y.

Narrow liquidity increased in 2023, even with the Fed reducing its securities holdings. The reduction in narrow liquidity from the Fed's System Open Market Account (SOMA) portfolio totaled just over \$0.9 trillion. But that was offset by over \$1.3 trillion in narrow liquidity injection from other sources. In 2023:

• The reverse repo program (O/N RRP) injected \$1.5 trillion of narrow liquidity

• The Bank Term Funding Program (BTFP) injected \$136 billion of narrow liquidity.

• The Treasury General Account (TGA) withdrew \$322bn of narrow liquidity.

Exhibit 5 and Exhibit 6 show how the major factors affecting narrow liquidity aside from the Fed's SOMA portfolio combined to dramatically increase the amount of narrow liquidity in 2023. This offset to the Fed's quantitative tightening (QT) efforts likely:

1. Allowed QT to proceed as smoothly as FOMC participants like to point out, and

2. Supported risk-taking and the financing of riskier assets, which

3. Prevented financial conditions from tightening further and helped them loosen.

The Year of the Dragon starts with only \$569bn available in the RRP – nowhere near the \$2.5 trillion available at the start of 2023. And the Fed will close the BTFP on March 11, at least until another round of unusual and exigent circumstances arise.

Meanwhile, fiscal policy likely will act as a drag on activity in 1H24, on the US Treasury's most recent financing need projections – suggesting \$540bn less financing need than 1H23 (-1.9pp of nominal GDP). So while economic data so far show no signs of fire, we see smoke on the horizon – and think investors should prepare by owning duration.

## Pin the Tail on the Dollar

As we discuss in United States | The increased confidence conundrum , changes in longend Treasury supply appear to have little bearing on auction tails. In Exhibit 7 and Exhibit 8 , we compare 10y and 30y auction tails to 12-month changes in auction sizes since 2010.

In both cases, changes in auction sizes have almost no explanatory power for auction outcomes. This result is robust to supply changes over shorter windows (3m, 6m) and regressions over different time frames since 2010.

Exhibit 7: 10y auction tails vs. 12m change in 10y auction sizes
![](images/e466c9b74676f0e258eab080a5376bd40810d8100a2e495afe42e591c13005d0.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, U.S. Treasury, Bloomberg

Exhibit 8: 30y auction tails vs. 12m change in 30y auction sizes
![](images/010683d7906578db8aa096da149115c21a575ba21ba0ef6a174d349546b2580b.jpg)

While changes in long-end supply may have little bearing on auction tails, 10y and 30y Treasury auctions matter for the USD.

Exhibit 9 and Exhibit 10 show the USD/JPY and USD/EUR correlation to moves in Treasury yields.

The yellow bars show how changes in USD/JPY and USD/EUR between 11am and 3pm correlate to changes in each tenor of Treasury yields between 11am and 3pm every day.

The blue bars show what that correlation looks like only on days when an auction of that tenor occurs.

For tenors where the yellow bars and the blue bars are the same, these results suggest that the USD-Treasury correlation is unchanged on auction days of that tenor.

• For example, USD/JPY is equally correlated to 5y Treasury yields on days when there is a 5y auction as on days when there is no 5y auction.

Whether or not a 5y auction occurs on a given day does not change USD/JPY's correlation to 5y Treasury yields on that day.

Exhibit 9: USD/JPY is particularly highly correlated to 10y and 30y yields on days when those tenors are auctioned...
![](images/b48cdfbe07ee0b14c207df1a98cba15b138c05131427a644e1ea69f6773f8cc7.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 10: ...as is the EUR
![](images/a50dac636561e6c18602bd1cd3e389551f60abbc7b930a2c0f7880e4a0033ad4.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

However, the charts indicate that both USD/JPY and USD/EUR become notably more highly correlated to 10y yields on days when a 10y auction occurs than on the average day. The same is true for 30y auction days and 30y yields.

These results suggest that 10y and 30y auctions matter for the USD in a way that other tenor auctions don't: the USD is particularly sensitive to yield moves driven by 10y and 30y auctions. See USD | Pin the tail on the Dollar for more discussion.

## Our Current Stance On Markets

In global rates markets, we maintain short 2y SOFR swap spread, long 5y UST, long 10s on 5s10s30s butterfly, and long Feb44 iota.

In the euro area, we close pay Apr ECB and long OAT Nov 32 yy ASW vs. EUR 6m vs. OAT May 53 yy ASW vs. EUR 6m. We enter receive EUR 5y5y (vs. 6m), short 0RM4 97.875/98.250 strangle, and short Bund ASW vs. Buxl ASW box. We maintain buy RXH4 136/137/138.5 broken call fly, receive EUR 5y5y swap (vs. 6m) versus EUR 20y5y swap (vs. 6m), short EU 3.25% 2034 on ASW, buy EU 0.7% 2051 vs. EU 2.5% 2052, short BTP 4.40% May ’33 vs. Bund 1.7% Aug ’32, and long OATei31.

In the UK, we maintain receive May ’24 MPC, long UKT 1T37vs. short UKT 1T57, sell 15 ASW, and long IL28.

In Japan, we enter long 20y JGBs on 10s30s30s fly. We maintain receive 2y TONA OIS outright, and SOFR/TONA basis 1y 4s9s flattener.

In foreign exchange markets, we close short GBK/NOK. We enter short CAD/NOK (target: 7.00, stop: 8.25). We maintain long NOK/SEK (target: 1.06, stop: 0.95) and short EUR/JPY (target: 145, stop: 162).

In FX options, we maintain long EUR/GBP 2-week calls and 1y USD/CHF 10-delta strangle (0.7775 put, 0.9550 call).

# Interest Rate Strategy

## United States

Long duration with tight stops: We remain cognizant of the risks to our long duration call, but think that Treasuries offer a good risk reward at these levels. With merely \~4 cuts priced through December, we think the market has ample scope to reverse this recent rise, and also make new lows in yields. In line with our "buy the dip" mantra, we continue to suggest long 5y notes, with stop out at 4.24%.

Four reasons why we like duration longs: (1) markets are ignoring inflation recovery and are focused on growth - like in 3Q23 - despite the Fed adding caveats on interpreting strong growth, (2) tactically our economists expect weak retail sales and decelerating CPI next week, (3) pushed out rate cuts can affect consumer and CEO sentiment negatively, creating a circular loop, and (4) valuations in real fed funds rate terms look attractive.

The sentiment-rates circularity that keeps a lid on yields: The recent consumer and business sentiment uplift can be seen as linked to consumers' and businesses' expectations of lower interest rates, and not necessarily an organic improvement. This circular loop between interest rates and confidence can limit how high yields can go - and is one reason why we see a good risk-reward profile for being long duration.

Another reminder of Treasury supply fallacy: While investors stay focus on Treasury supply, the 10y and 30y auctions this week stopped through 1.2bp and 2.0bp - a total of 3.2bp. The overall relationship between auction performance and rise in supply is very weak. This analysis is a timely reminder that (1) supply matters when expectations are formed and not when supply actually increases, and (2) macro conditions will outweigh supply considerations.

Curves back to "normal", but we prefer staying away: We update our regime selection model for the 2s30s, 2s10s, and 5s30s curves. All three curves have now reached "normal" regimes, making the environment more conducive to curve trades. However, we still see curve steepeners as highly subscribed, and suggest sticking to duration longs instead of steepeners.

## Euro area

On duration, we highlight how, without a sizeable uptick in inflation expectations, the total amount of cuts priced in should not be repriced materially higher. A reconvergence towards a 2% depo should be warranted, particularly if the spot disinflation process persists. Given the directionality between the front-end and duration, we think that it is more the distribution of cuts that is subject to being repriced, given the front-loaded nature of market pricing. Given the re-pricing already seen, we believe outright duration exposure offers a good entry point. We receive EUR 5y5y (v 6m).

At the front end of the market, as highlighted last week, we think that the terminal rate pricing should hover around the 2% level for the foreseeable future. The recent sell-off brought the trough priced into the strip to \~2.15% (adjusted for the

FRA/ESTR basis). All in all, we think this calls for the priced terminal rate to hover around 2% for the next few months or until cutting cycles start. We recommend positioning for this outcome by selling the Jun 24 1y midcurve 97.875/98.250 strangle, receiving a net credit of \~47cents.

On ASW, we note that following last week's last round of syndications on the 30y point, long-end issuance is set to subside meaningfully over the coming months. We close our short 30y OAT ASW vs 10y and rotate into RX/UB invoice spreads steepeners.

## United Kingdom

A busy week ahead on the data front will likely determine whether or not the recent sell-off has further to go, given that market sentiment will likely depend on any macro data surprise. Our economist expects a technical recession, an uptick in services inflation and another solid drop in headline pay growth, which will bring a complex mix of data, and is likely to set the tone for the March MPC meeting.

The other relevant theme has been a broad flattening in UK rates, and the repricing lower in easing expectations has likely been the main culprit, while the limited supply ahead has further supported the flattening pressure. Our medium-term view for more steepening ahead remains intact as far as the BoE's easing expectations for the current year stay in place, which is a more relevant factor when it comes to the yield curve drivers compared to supply considerations.

With respect to upcoming Budget, we expect another meaningful increase in the gilt remit for FY2024/25 to be delivered, and an increase in shorts and mediums at the expense of longs. However, the main risk come from a bucket split that surprises market expectations in terms of more longs and less shorts in a higherthan-expected gilt remit, which can limit any reduction in longs in cash and DV01 terms, which eventually matters for valuations.

## Japan

We discuss the market implications of BoJ deputy governor Uchida's speech on February 8. While he didn’t signal any clear hints for normalization on the upcoming March MPM, he explained the basic thinking regarding potential revisions to individual measures. The takeaway is that there is no reason to expect a faster (than “very gradual”) pace of BoJ rate hikes under current economic and inflation circumstances.

We would now contend that a significantly more hawkish rate hike trajectory is unlikely to be priced in absent some major change in exogenous circumstances, such as a renewed acceleration in inflation or another higher US yield (and higher USD/JPY). We believe that any bearish structure for BoJ's normalization would be expressed a little further out on the curve in (say) the 5y–10y sector in anticipation for a "slower but longer scenario".

We maintain receiving 2y OIS outright position, and sell 30y JGB ASW. We newly enter long 20y JGBs on 10s20s30s fly as a bullish duration expression. We also discuss whether the 5y and 10y GX bonds will have to be issued as premium.

## United States | The increased confidence conundrum

## A step towards increased confidence

Treasury yields had an upward drift over the last week, without much significant data or surprising Fedspeak. The data for the week included CPI revisions which should help the Fed get "increased confidence" that the realized inflation progress is being maintained, an ISM number that appeared stronger on the surface, and jobless claims that remained low. Overall, while data had mixed signals, the market traded with an upward drift in yields as the path of last resistance after last week's payroll report.

Exhibit 11: Move in yields and breakevens over the last week
![](images/90a9f0569426c7312f37c946c31a3993295d2f1afc191c7bd71e6c2c081c6b64.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 12: 3m rolling core CPI path before and after revisions
![](images/eb5b7350dca148a7be1de4d54911620bc7206a29fbe9c98541db4b5a35bcfbc0.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

We remain cognizant of the risks to our long duration call, but think that Treasuries offer a good risk-reward at these levels. With merely 4 cuts priced into the next 12 months (see Exhibit 13 ), we think the market has ample scope to reverse this recent rise, and also make new lows in yields. In line with our "buy the dip" mantra, we stay long duration – we continue to suggest long 5y notes, with stop out at 4.24%.

We see four reasons to stay with duration longs (1) markets are ignoring inflation recovery and are focused on growth - like in 3Q23 - despite the Fed adding caveats on interpreting strong growth (see Exhibit 14 and grey box), (2) tactically, our economists expect weak retail sales and decelerating CPI next week, (3) pushed out rate cuts can affect consumer and CEO sentiment negatively, creating a circular loop, and (4) valuations in real fed funds rate terms look attractive.

Powell on the importance of growth data, January 2024 FOMC meeting (our emphasis in bold): Continued declines in inflation are really the main thing we are looking at. Of course, we want the labor market to remain strong, too. We have a maximum mandate and price stability mandate. Growth only matters to the extent it influences our achievement of those two mandates.

![](images/39ccc670c50ea941f85f39209069c078f6fd67bad05f875c0c8ebf068ffd49fb.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 14: 10y Treasury yield changes vs. inflation and growth surprises
![](images/bb6ead963e2786dfa92353ab5aa799144a1a52d48a6616c6293cab24e185136b.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

## Will pushed out rate cuts lead to cooling sentiment?

Recent weeks saw an improvement in consumer sentiment - both consumer sentiment in the University of Michigan survey and Conference Board survey showed notable upticks. Additionally, the latest Conference Board CEO confidence survey shows a reading above 50 for the first time since 1Q22. It is important to the circular loop that exists between confidence in the economy and the expected path of interest rates.

The direct question in the University of Michigan survey that asks consumers about the expected path of interest rates rose sharply since December (see Exhibit 15 ), which also coincided with a significant jump in future expectations (and a smaller uptick in the current conditions). Similarly, the Conference Board Survey registered an uptick in sentiment alongside a big increase in expectation of lower rates (see Exhibit 16 ).

The Conference Board survey on the CEO confidence also noted that 62% of the CEOs think that the biggest opportunity in 2024 could revolve around lower interest rates and lower inflation.

Exhibit 15: University of Michigan consumer sentiment survey and expectations of interest rates
![](images/65a6202c580ebdce0d2b82d711713845ca11216e32432d43fc256ccb3e2552ea.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, University of Michigan, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 16: Conference Board consumer confidence survey and expectations of interest rates
![](images/aab0228fdee506e24d9ec6431996bb86415191b4053180c9a4c574789ab6fd6b.jpg)
Source: Conference Board, Morgan Stanley Research

In a similar vein, consumer expectations can also be linked to market expectations of cooling interest rates (see Exhibit 17 ), a relationship that seems more consistent than interest rates expectations in the survey itself. As markets remove rate cuts in the near term, the consumer sentiment could reverse some of its upswing, and lead to weaker data in the coming weeks.

Said differently, the recent consumer and business sentiment uplift can be seen as linked to consumers' and businesses' expectations of lower interest rates and not necessarily an organic improvement.

This circular loop between interest rates and confidence can be a limit on how high yields can go before they threaten the recent recovery in sentiment - and is one reason why we see a good risk-reward profile for being long duration.

Exhibit 17: University of Michigan consumer sentiment vs. market pricing of interest rates
![](images/5a221c3e51fee8f33bf6226c05fbbe210328c37f6ef36f542e401dc29bc02afd.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, University of Michigan, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 18: CEO optimism index over the last eight years
![](images/52c2ed0cbd3012f0b7c1c3c65ffb2e2f81e783d3639d7cb3d197cab44b77b1bc.jpg)
Source: Source: Conference Board, Haver, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 19: Core CPI implied by fixings vs. real fed funds rate implied by market for 12m
![](images/0dbe4d5b99780ea4c6db8343e83aef001e4fb29364ea8f64a908d5fd7dbe8d41.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Finally, we also see the recent uptick in the market-implied real fed funds rate, backed out from fed funds rate implied by the market and subtracting the CPI fixings implied core inflation. The projected real fed funds rate keeps going higher (see Exhibit 19 ) in a period of inflation progress, which ultimately can be seen as a continued increase in restriction, despite a Fed cutting cycle on the horizon. We think there is ample scope to fade this uptick in the real fed funds rate - another reason we like long duration.

## Assessing the impact of Treasury supply - insights from auctions

The 10y and 30y auctions this week stopped through 1.2bp and 2.0bp – a total of 3.2bp stop through – rarely seen in the last fifteen years. These auctions contrast with the large tails in fall 2023 that were seen as concerns about the impact of Treasury supply on yields. While discussions about supply were prevalent in the fall, when long-end yields were higher than they are today, these discussions have faded as yields have fallen.

In light of the strong long-end auctions this week and fewer recent discussions of supply, we investigate how much changes in long-end

supply actually impact yields at auctions. In Exhibit 20 and Exhibit 21 , we compare 10y and 30y auction tails to 12-month changes in auction sizes since 2010. In both cases, changes in auction sizes have almost no explanatory power for auction outcomes. This result is robust to supply changes over shorter windows (3m, 6m) and regressions over different timeframes since 2010.

Exhibit 20: 10y auction tails vs. 12m change in 10y auction sizes
![](images/f3c8d4d8031d6e5748e85400b9d62b282736600f881d0c133a768cd4915f81d1.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, U.S. Treasury, Bloomberg

Exhibit 21: 30y auction tails vs. 12m change in 30y auction sizes
![](images/1cb9e35055dc7830d9b4a029659b4e666163ac8b7ed033fc9b2d32bfaea328b5.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, U.S. Treasury, Bloomberg

These results do not necessarily mean that supply has no bearing on yields. Rather, it highlights two things (1) that Treasury supply matters a lot more when expectations about supply are forming, like the fall of 2023, and not when the Treasury supply actually arrives, and (2) these results also indicate that rising supply can be peripheral to macro factors, which we have previously discussed, and on its own is not reason enough to go short bonds.

## Revisiting curve regimes and prospects for steepeners

In a previous publication, we introduced the idea of normal and abnormal curve regimes for the 2s30s curve. We defined the normal regime as when the curve is primarily driven by front end-led movements, such as bull steepening and bear flattening, and the abnormal regime as when the curve is driven by the long end via bear steepening or bull flattening.

Our rule for determining when 2s30s was in an abnormal regime is whether the percentage of day-over-day abnormal changes in the curve (by magnitude) over the past 3 months exceeds 50%. Furthermore, we require that the percentage stay above or below 50% for five days in order for the regime to switch between abnormal/normal.

We expand our analysis to the 5s30s and 2s10s curves. To select thresholds, we identify the 60th percentile of the 3-month percentage of long-end led movements going back to 2000 – excluding periods when the Fed is at the effective lower bond (ELB). This rule gives us our 50% threshold for 2s30s, a 38% threshold for 5s30s, and a 58% threshold for 2s10s.

In Exhibit 22 , Exhibit 23 , and Exhibit 24 , we show, over trailing 3-month windows, the percentage of day-over-day curve movements led by the long end and highlight abnormal regimes. In Exhibit 25 , we present current indicators for the three curves, showing the percentage of long end-led curve movements, whether we are current in an abnormal regime, and when we last entered and exited an abnormal regime.

Exhibit 22: Percentage of 2s30s movements led by the long end with regime indicators
![](images/d348398f12842ef257e23451ddbd48f6f12bdb86c37a0a7f9e7ca1dc05b833b8.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg

Exhibit 23: Percentage of 5s30s movements led by the long end with regime indicators
![](images/03ed93f08a9cb0fcc151ee96842ac77c0647ad5f1cd20beeb04acfdd1458517e.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg

Exhibit 24: Percentage of 2s10s movements led by the long end with regime indicators
![](images/6cdda997499b8e6bde37eff33b66102852f61b8894580c4f0b90e505bc911959.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg

Exhibit 25: Current regime indicators for the 2s10s, 2s30s, and 5s30s curves
<table><tr><td>Curve</td><td>% Long- End Move ments</td><td>Thres Abnor hold</td><td>Entere mal</td><td>Last Last Exited d</td></tr><tr><td>2s10s</td><td>46.1%</td><td>58%</td><td>No</td><td>10-Oct- 17-Jan- 23 24</td></tr><tr><td>2s30s</td><td>39.6%</td><td>50%</td><td>No</td><td>29-Sep- 16-Jan- 23</td></tr><tr><td>5s30s</td><td>34.4%</td><td>38%</td><td>No</td><td>24 29-Sep- 8-Feb- 23 24</td></tr></table>

Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg

Our analysis indicates that we have exited abnormal regimes for all three curves, making the environment better suited to curve trades in general. The 2s10s and 2s30s in particular have been in the normal regime for weeks, and their percentages of long-endled movements appear well below their respective abnormality thresholds ( Exhibit 25 ), while the 5s30s curve recently exited abnormal regime.

Despite better environment for curve trades, we still believe that curve steepener trades are widely consensus, and we continue to suggest duration longs over curve steepeners.

• Trade idea: Maintain long 5s at 4.15% with a stop out at 4.24%

This week the market continued to adjust its expectations to the hawkish side - a US-led move following last week’s data. 2024 cuts in OIS contracts now stand at around \~-115bp (4.6 cuts of 25bp) up from -150bp (6 cuts of 25bp) in early January. This lifted the pricing of the terminal rate depo to above the 2% level, with the trough in OIS contracts standing at around \~2.15%.

As such, the curve continued to flatten as the market re-assesses the pricing at the front end. Looking ahead, the data calendar in Europe will still be pretty light with most of the risk stemming from the UK, where we will have the wage data release on the 13th (MS total pay %3m/Y forecast: 5.7%, last :6.5%) along with the Jan CPI print on the 14th (MS: 4% y/y, C: 4.1%). In the US, Jan CPI will be in the spotlight, where our economics team in the US expects a deceleration in core to 0.26% m/m in Jan-24 (vs 0.31%M Dec-23, 0.3%M cons).

On the issuance side, as highlighted last week, the supply schedule will be light; we expect €98.5bn of supply in the next five weeks against €17.2bn in coupons and €102.7bn in redemptions, resulting in net issuance of -€21.4bn.

On the positioning front, the biggest portion of shorts outstanding is predominantly on the 10y contract, where market agents have been running \~-10.3mn/01 on the contract, adding -1.6mn/01 shorts during the last week, equal to 82% of the total max-short positioning run over the past 6 months (see Exhibit 29 ). As a reminder, the data from Eurex are on the German futures contract, and as a such part of these metrics may be polluted by short ASW positions or basis trades.

As extensively highlighted in previous iterations of the GMS (see here and here), these days it is the front-end of the market that drives the moves in duration. This is very visible both by looking at the importance of the factors behind our 10y Bund model (see Exhibit 30 ) and by looking at how duration reacts for changes in the cumulative ECB pricing (see Exhibit 31 ).

Anecdotally, we tend to think that inflation expectations are still the key underlying variable, as a premature easing into an economy that grows more strongly than expected can reinforce the rhetoric of second-round effects on inflation, and hence the potential risks of easing too early.

We find some evidence to support this by looking at the relationship between cumulative priced ECB cuts vs inflation expectations (see Exhibit 32 ). We can see how, following the December rally, the expectation on total cuts was wrong-footed relative the level of inflation break-even (see Exhibit 33 ).

Source: Bloomberg, ECB, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 32: Cumulative cuts priced vs Inflation expectations.
![](images/8a68dd4586c4790c3bbdbb216d319b60c445a4f4628e1c1287bfc842bf343f84.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 33: HICP X-Tob path vs MS forecasts, fixings, and ECB quarterly forecast
![](images/66e558e071fdb133d28664a20dd963ab60cf32f59a3054309ab5829ab2023160.jpg)

While a surge in HICP 5y5y cannot be excluded, we tend to think that for the time being this remains more of a tail risk, for the following reasons:

• 5y5y HICP remains extremely stable, having been stuck in a \~10bp range since the beginning of the year.

• The fixings profile has not changed dramatically, despite the upbeat in core and headline Jan print.

• The ECB’s quarterly projections for its inflation profile are way higher than market pricing (or our economists’ profile, see Exhibit 33 ). As such, the March meeting could pave the way for a mark-down in the ECB’s revised forecasts, predominantly in the headline projections, given the drop in gas prices since its previous cut-off period for the GC December’s projection (see our economists' take on this matter here).

• As such, the market will slowly need to factor a lower “inflation-premia” over time, which is currently hovering in the \~20/30bp region by simply looking at the difference between 5y5y HICP and the results on 5y inflation expectations from the ECB survey of professional forecaster (see Exhibit 34 ).

Exhibit 34: 5y5y Inflation Swap vs ECB’s Survey of Professional forecaster
![](images/b2613f522c2bb031b710937c62c18ae92a12f9525dfba0b897f302564725eb54.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, ECB,Morgan Stanley Research

Put differently - without inflation expectations picking up sustainably, and on the premise that long-dated inflation forwards are already incorporating a “premium”, the cumulative amount of cuts priced won’t be repriced materially higher, in our view. At this point of the cycle, while the starting point for cuts remains open for debate, we know that the next step in monetary policy is some form of easing.

Exhibit 35: Distribution of cuts priced in Euribor calendar spreads until Dec 25- RHS: break-down of cuts priced (as% of total Mar 24/Dec 25 cuts priced in)
![](images/ab12743be92617574cd27f259bad403453647def1e536b77b24321fc5b1598f1.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Additionally, despite the uncertainties on R\* estimates, we know that a re-convergence towards the 2% depo should be warranted in the next couple of years, particularly if the spot disinflation process persists.

As such, in case of further sell-off, we think that it is more the distribution of cuts that is subject to being repriced, given the front-loaded nature of market pricing in Exhibit 35 , we can see that 50% of the total amount of cuts priced to Dec 25 is expected to happen between Q2/Q3 2024) as opposed to the total cumulative number.

This is relevant because it allows us to establish indicative boundaries on duration based on the total amount of cuts priced, and on where will depo consequently settle (see Exhibit 36 ).

On this premise, we think duration offers a good entry point at these levels. For the time being, ECB terminal rate pricing is likely to hover around current levels, with potential for a faster re-pricing lower, whilst the realization of the ECB terminating above 2.50% seems more of a tail risk.

We receive EUR 5y5y currently at 2.76%, with a first target at 2.51%. Stop 2.90%.

The risks to the trade include a faster acceleration in inflation, and widening break-evens, leading to a sustained repricing of the cumulative amount of cuts expected in this cycle.

## Front end - Enter 1y midcurve Jun 24 97.875/98.250 strangle

Last week in Don't Stop Receiving, we showed that:

(i) Markets in previous ECB easing cycles tended to underestimate the amount of cuts four months before the first cut. Markets started pricing more cuts the moment they had reassurance that the easing cycle had started, i.e., once the ECB had delivered the first cut. Nonetheless, market expectations at the start of previous ECB cycles were still understating the cumulative amount of easing.

In Exhibit 37 , we plot the ECB rate path implied by forward OIS four months before the first cut in each cycle vs what was the actual level of the base rate (MRO or depo whichever was more relevant) upon the forwards' expiry. In all instances, markets were underestimating the total amount of cuts. In particular, one-year ahead the ECB main rate ended at least 100bp+ and up to 350bp+ below market expectations.

Exhibit 37: Four months before the first cut, markets underestimated the total amount of cuts in the coming year
![](images/5638b76d6c91e9195220330ae9158c43c99e076814ec4ced505c56afe4960b19.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research; Note: forward OIS (eonia) adjusted for spread eonia/depo or mro

(ii) But that doesn’t mean more cuts should be priced in the current context, at least not until the ECB delivers the first cut in June as expected by our economists, and not unless the macro picture changes meaningfully. Effectively, for this cycle, markets are pricing a lot more cuts now than they were in previous cycles - in the 2008 and 2011 cycles markets were even pricing hikes (see Exhibit 38 which shows the ECB path implied by forwards OIS four months before the start of the easing cycle). Similar conclusions can be made on the day of the first cut, where the magnitude of cuts priced was relatively small compared to the current pricing (see Exhibit 39 ).

Exhibit 38: Markets are pricing more cuts now than they were in previous cycles
![](images/3d33a088d7dcd8b85f4b3d58478215eb55d952096a406cd2938f0ee2145d6acc.jpg)

![](images/f523d8e238302d59959a65ddd8f13a36a62ef387c5c2e2ba276b8bb5663e682d.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

We also see little room for more cuts to be priced out. Exhibit 40 shows the trough in the Euribor strip adjusted for the FRA/€str basis. Since the January FOMC and NFPs, the priced terminal rate has risen back 20bp to 2.15%, early December levels. As mentioned in the duration section, without having inflation expectations picking up sustainabily, it will be difficult for markets to price less cumulative cuts, in our view. Additionally, strength in inflation data would likely drive more whites than reds and the terminal rate priced.

Exhibit 40: The trough in the Euribor strip rose back to 2.15%.
![](images/eb01904f2eb5117dd1fd84d5201adaf42948bae00b52f7e4a27c1bb1c23467c9.jpg)

Source: Pricing Monkey, Morgan Stanley Research

All in all, we think this calls for the priced terminal rate to hover around 2% for the next few months or until cutting cycles start. We recommend selling 0RM4 97.875/98.250 strangle (Euribor Jun24 1y midcurve), currently at \~47cts (see Exhibit 41 ).The potential loss is unlimited, with the main risk being a rebound in inflation or strength in growth data as well as a sharp deterioration in the macro outlook, which would warrant a meaningful repricing lower or higher of ERM5. An increase in volatility would also be detrimental to the trade. For investors who favour a limited loss structures, the 97.750/97.875/98.250/98.375 iron condor could be an alternative, with similar risks applying.

With respect to front meetings, in OIS the April ECB meeting now trades at -13bp, implying a \~50% probability of a cut. With a few risk events next week (see Duration section above) and the ECB likely to maintain its data dependent stance for now, the April meeting seems fairly priced. Thus we close pay April ECB meeting.

## ASW - Enter RX/UB Invoice spread box steepeners

Over the past three weeks, core ASW have been relatively rangy with: (i) the widening seasonality; and (ii) the deterioration of risk sentiment during a few sessions (on the back of concerns over banks' exposure to CRE) preventing ASW from cheapening further (see Exhibit 42 ).

Positioning remains short. Lately, the intra-day risk-off highlighted positioning remained very skewed to shorts in ASW. For instance, Eurex statistics showed a reduction of more than €4mn/01 of shorts over the four German contracts (including €2.2mn/01) on the 31 January. It is likely these shorts were ASW trades given the discrepancy between price action on duration and the change in positioning, as Exhibit 43 illustrates.

Another recent theme has been the flattening of the Bund/Buxl box, which we played indirectly with the 32s53s OAT ASW box (short 53s) (see Don't Call It A Comeback). The Buxl ASW led the box flatter, as shown in Exhibit 44 , amid heavy supply in the 30y sector. This also reflects in a very high correlation between the Buxl box and the Bund/ Buxl ASW box.

The correlation between the box and the Buxl ASW tends to increase with the supply ratio however recently the ratio and correlation have diverged. The correlation hovered around 90% since June despite a decline in the long-end supply relative to that in the 10y sector. This is reflective of the Buxl ASW trading in a more idiosyncratic fashion even when supply is not in focus.

Exhibit 44: The Buxl ASW led the box Bund/Buxl ASW flatter
![](images/e9f2b80b39b7ca5f3056969e80c68381ba585ff0e6a779e3df6d689b3d1ab90d.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 45: The correlation between the box and the Buxl ASW increases with the supply ratio
![](images/b99d8c6002640c4a7b9a8a046207eaae696890f273046791c218b4883f0b752c.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research; Note: correlation of daily changes

Long-end supply to subside meaningfully. Supply dynamics should switch from here; after \~€25mn/01 issued per week in the 15y+ sector over the past four weeks in EA10, we expect the 15y+ supply to amount to only €9.1mn/01 for the rest of February, including €3.5mn/01 in the 30y sector. Compared to previous years, this is substantially lower with an average €39mn/01 of issuance remaining for the month of February (see Exhibit 46 ). In our opinion, this should support a steepening of the Bund/Buxl ASW box in the coming weeks.

Exhibit 46: We expect the 15y+ supply to amount to only €9.1mn/01 for the rest of February
![](images/a69a222d39680af154cb5881ff62467595e5d5119dfa00c862c1a04e699cd7ba.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research estimates

<table><tr><td>ASW vs EUR 6m</td><td>Schatz</td><td>Bobl</td><td>Bund</td><td>Buxl</td></tr><tr><td>Observed (bp)</td><td>39.4</td><td>44.3</td><td>42.9</td><td>8.3</td></tr><tr><td>Fair Value (bp)</td><td>52.7</td><td>52.2</td><td>48.7</td><td>17.0</td></tr><tr><td>Gap to Fair Value (bp)</td><td>-13.3</td><td>-7.9</td><td>-5.8</td><td>-8.7</td></tr><tr><td>Z score (Gap to Fair Value)</td><td>-1.2</td><td>-1.1</td><td>-0.9</td><td>-1.3</td></tr></table>

In terms of valuation, the Bund ASW and the Buxl ASW are 6bp and 9bp cheap, respectively. The 3bp excessive cheapness of the Buxl ASW highlights the box is too flat, a result of the heavy supply, in our opinion (the fair values remained relatively stable this past month). This also pleads for a re-steepening of the box. Finally, the seasonal widening of the Bund ASW ended on the 9 February. Should the protracted cheapening of ASW resume - which should be the trend in the medium term, see Don't Call It A Comeback - the box should steepen.

On the OAT front, the 10s30s slope is roughly in line with our model fair value while the 10y OAT ASW is \~3bp rich. With the next long-end supply only in March, we think the risk/reward for the 32s53s OAT ASW box (short 53s) is not as attractive, hence we close it and switch to a Bund/Buxl ASW box steepener (long Buxl ASW), currently around -36bp, with a first target at -30bp. Stop at -38bp. The main risk to the trade is a deterioration of risk sentiment and the short positioning in ASW.

What about EU ASW ?

SSA - Maintain short EU 34s on ASW. In the SSA space, we recommended selling EU 3.25% 2034 on ASW in EU 2024 Issuance – A Few Factors Could Tip the Balance to position for the OAT ASW December seasonal cheapening and given our initial estimated target of €90bn of issuance for 1H24. The EU 34s ASW failed to cheapen in December as OAT ASW outperformed the Bund ASW in the peripheral and semi-core spread tightening (see Exhibit 48 Exhibit 7 ).

Since the beginning of the year, EU 34s ASW cheapened back alongside the cheapening of OAT ASW and as semi-core spreads seemed to have found a floor. As mentioned in Don't Call It A Comeback, our medium-term view is that of a cheapening of core ASW in 2024. Despite not being our base case, in the short term, the main risk would be a sustained deterioration of risk sentiment. In such a scenario, being short EU 34s ASW presents a better risk/reward than short OAT or Bund ASW given: (i) EU should underperform core and semi-core names in a risk-off, in our view; and (ii) positioning is likely cleaner.

Exhibit 48: EU 34s ASW versus 10y OAT ASW
![](images/c8f2f365b5dd7e849e6876c9fe4c3de2033bc49628886ca569b0fb84ae2ddc68.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 49: EU issued for €17bn ytd, representing 23% of its €75bn target
![](images/9f8379260c631f52a26c5341d94db78bb259241e5adceb72d755ac187e544585.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Finally, EU issued for €17bn ytd, representing 23% of its €75bn target. Funding progress as of end of January was similar to that of 2023 and 2021 (see Exhibit 8 ), however the remaining \~€58bn to issue in 1H could weigh on EU vs. peers. For the reasons mentioned above, we maintain short EU 34s on ASW (vs. 6m).

• Trade idea: Enter short Bund ASW vs Buxl ASW box . Currently at - 36bp. First target at -30bp. Stop -38bp.

• Trade idea: Maintain sell EU 3.25% 2034 on ASW. Target at 30bp. Stop at 19bp

• Trade idea: Close 10s30s OAT ASW box, i.e., long OAT Nov 32 yy ASW vs EUR 6m vs OAT May 53 yy ASW vs EUR 6m.

## A busy week ahead, with macro data releases to set market sentiment

The BoE's easing expectations readjusted significantly lower over the past week and came back to pre-December 2023 FOMC levels, with \~75bp of cuts being priced in for the current year and less than -150bp priced in until December 2025 (see Exhibit 52 ).

The move in the BoE's easing expectations has followed the global move, with a solid US jobs data report more recently shifting market sentiment towards a more hawkish stance. UK duration sold off during the past week (see Exhibit 53 ), with the five-year sector underperforming on the curve as the most sensitive to a repricing lower in BoE easing expectations despite a relatively quite week on the data front.

However, BoE speakers have been watched closely and the main takeaways relate to Mann's speech and Haskel's interview, which both hinted that the decision for hiking rates at the last MPC was finely balanced while service inflation and wage growth developments, respectively, remain the critical factors that might or might not support a voting shift towards holding rates at the next MPC.

Exhibit 52: The market readjusted the BoE's easing expectations lower to levels seen before the December FOMC
![](images/7dabd77ffee788673fa3aa231f3a78338ccddcd03eae795a0b2445edd1019f63.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 53: UK duration sold off, with the belly of the curve underperforming in a broad flattening move
![](images/a373f07f64b6c0922f1c0d815db5ccd01e1d37e1bdade40c2e1b81e340deaa71.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

The upcoming week will then be crucial for UK rates as wage growth data, the January Inflation Report, GDP data and retail sales will be released. In A Busy Data Week Ahead, our economist expects a technical recession, an uptick in services inflation and another solid drop in headline pay growth, which will bring a complex mix of data, and is likely to set the tone for the March MPC meeting.

While duration has cheapened over the past week and easing expectations repriced significantly below our modal view, the upcoming data will determine whether or not the recent sell-off has further room to go – given that market sentiment will likely depend on any macro data surprise. In addition, a more resilient US economy and the related risks for a reacceleration of inflation pose continuing risks to UK duration, and so we maintain a neutral stance ahead of the upcoming busy week.

The other relevant theme has been a broad flattening in UK rates in sympathy with other markets. The repricing lower in easing expectations was likely the main culprit, while the limited supply ahead for the remainder of the current fiscal year (see Exhibit 54 ) has further supported the flattening pressure. In addition, expectations for the upcoming remit – with a shift towards more shorts and mediums and less longs, which is largely expected – could have further explained the recent move.

While more flattening is possible in the short term should next week's data surprise on the upside, our medium-term view for more steepening ahead remains intact as far as the BoE's easing expectations for the current year stay in place; this is a more relevant factor when it comes to the yield curve drivers compared to supply considerations. On top of that, the recent sell-off in front-end rates reduces the scope for a further flattening.

Also, we tend to disagree with the narrative that UK rates have over-steepened in the last months as this does not seem true when looking at previous BoE cycles, and historical patterns confirm our view for more steepening ahead (see Exhibit 55 ).

Lastly, the upcoming Budget poses two-way risks on the shape of the curve. While a shift in supply towards less longs and a lower weighted average maturity is expected, this is also possibly already priced in and thus the extent of the shift in supply compared to market expectations should determine the reaction in the shape of the yield curve.

In this regard, we see the tail risk being a higher-than-expected gilt supply which overall limits any substantial reduction in longs in cash terms and DV01 (that eventually matter for valuations), given that electoral incentives are such that a meaningful under-spend is unlikely.

Exhibit 54: The supply backdrop is more supportive in the coming weeks, with 30y supply only in the linkers space
![](images/7f49b94213c4f76f77032e2f7a5a5ba49905550b8a63bd5e453c0d7a1c8ba80f.jpg)
Source: UK DMO, Morgan Stanley Research estimates

Exhibit 55: Has the UK curve over-steepened since the BoE paused hiking rates? From a historical point of view, not really
![](images/053fd779daae94f70d75018806219e20905d81790532515fee56d56a65b93714.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

## Budget preview – more mediums into a bigger-than-ever gilt supply

Content from the section below was previously published here.

For March in our Budget Preview: Delaying the Inevitable, our economist puts the fiscal space at £23 billion and assumes \~£15 billion of easing/year, largely via (income) tax cuts. She assumes that tax cuts will be largely reversed post-November to fund the likely Spending Review increase in current expenditure. In the long run, the fiscal picture looks challenging if rates remain structurally higher.

With respect to gilt supply, we expect another meaningful increase in the gilt remit for FY2024/25 to be delivered at the upcoming Budget – largely as expected by the market – and the maturity split is a crucial factor for the evolution of the gilt market. Both GEMMs and investors pointed to a lesser need for high-DV01 instruments and thus advocated for a shift towards more shorts and mediums according to the minutes of the annual consultation with the DMO published on January 29, 2024, in order to meet a structural shift in demand for gilts.

Demand for longs and especially ultras has remained subdued in recent years – a pattern that will likely persist going forward given the UK defined benefit pension landscape and its trends. More and more defined benefit pension schemes will be closed to new members and benefit accrual and thus the average liability duration of the schemes should continue to decrease as they mature.

We think that the DMO will take into account this shift in demand at the upcoming gilt remit while considering the increasing refinancing risk which results from lowering the weighted average maturity of the gilt supply and stock.

According to our estimates, CGNCR (ex NRAM, B&B and NR) for FY2024/25 will be at \~ £144.5 billion, which brings the Gross Financing Requirement to \~£276 billion (including £139.9 billion of redemptions) and takes into account this year's fiscal outperformance of \~£8.4 billion versus the November OBR's projections.In Exhibit 56 and Exhibit 57 we report our issuance estimates, breakdown and comparison versus the FY2023/24 Spring Budget figures.

Exhibit 56: FY2024/25 GFR estimates and breakdown

Source: UK DMO, Morgan Stanley Research estimates

When it comes to gilt issuance, we pencil in total gross gilt supply at \~£257 billion, up from FY2023/24's supply at \~£237 billion, which was revised lower at the Autumn Statement compared to the 2023 Budget (reported in Exhibit 57 ).

We expect a split towards more mediums and less longs in order to accommodate the shift in demand. We think that shorts will remain at \~36% of total gilt supply – unchanged from the previous year as their contribution is already at all-time highs and the DMO might avoid increasing that even further in order to keep refinancing risk under control. In addition, as the overall supply should increase, shorts supply should also increase in cash terms if the contribution in percentage terms is kept unchanged.

We pencil in mediums at \~29% of the total gross gilt issuance, up from the previous fiscal year, which will absorb much of the additional issuance. The contribution of longs and linkers will likely be revised lower to 19.5% and 10%, respectively.

We also think that the unallocated portion will be revised up slightly to \~5.5% of the overall remit as a way to retain more flexibility for gilt supply. It is worth noting that our estimate breakdown will translate to higher or almost unchanged supply across all the buckets compared to FY2023/24 in cash terms (see Exhibit 57 ), given that total supply is expected to increase. Hence, the reduction in the contribution of longs and mediums probably won't translate into lower supply of these lines in cash and DV01 terms – which eventually matter for valuations.

According to our estimates in Exhibit 57 , we see \~£92.5 billion of shorts and \~£74.5 billion of mediums for a total contribution of \~65% in percentage terms – similar to FY2020/21, where gross issuance (excluding QE) came in at all-time highs (\~£485 billion) during the pandemic and shorts and mediums accounted for \~35% and \~30%, respectively.

We then factor in longs at £50.1 billion, which remains a historical high number despite the reduction in percentage terms, and similarly we expect \~£25.7 billion for linkers, almost unchanged versus the current fiscal year. Linker issuance should remain well below the previous historical norm of \~20%, but we do not expect a decline below 10% given likely structural demand for linkers in the region of £25-30 billion per year and the commitment from the DMO to continue with the linkers issuance programme.

We also expect the unallocated portion at \~£14.1 billion – higher than in FY2023/24 while in line with its historical contribution in percentage terms. Since 2012 this portion has been set at 5.3% on average at the Spring Budget, excluding FY2021/22 when the pandemic required higher-than-ever flexibility. It is also likely that longs and linkers supply will eventually be higher than the initial projections suggest, as the unallocated portion has historically been used mostly for these lines, which require more flexibility. This is also something to keep in mind when considering the initial bucket split impact on longs and linkers valuations.

## NS&I and T-bills considerations

For the NS&I contribution we pencil in a target of £9 billion, up slightly from the FY2023/24 target at £7.5 billion, which we believe is realistic given the following observations. On the one hand, NS&I demand could pick up given the attractive rate of saving products and the improving real wage growth supporting demand for saving products.

On the other hand, saving rates are likely to plateau in the upcoming year while remortgaging should continue to weigh on households' cash flows and savings. Hence, we do not expect a target significantly higher than in FY2023/24 (£7.5 billion) as the effective demand for NS&I products remains uncertain – and the risk of under-delivering should prevent the target from being set significantly above the historical target and actual contribution levels. In this regard, the actual NS&I contribution over the past years has varied over time, averaging \~£12 billion since 2016, while the initial target has been set most of the time at £6 billion with a +/-£3 billion range (see Exhibit 58 ).

The only significant outlier has been during FY2020/21, where the actual contribution overshot the initial target substantially as savings accumulated during the pandemic supported demand for NS&I products – a scenario which is unlikely to repeat, in our view.

On the T-bills, we factor in a net contribution of £10 billion for FY2024/25 with the following considerations. The stock of T-bills outstanding is at historically high levels, as shown in Exhibit 59 (\~£73 billion at the end of January 2024), with the DMO unwilling to increase it much further in order to retain some flexibility for cash-management purposes.

Exhibit 58: Historical NS&I target and effective contribution
![](images/b66501b171fb00c45f6d045781e0070ea98ce6df55b07b1fba683f78c4b85190.jpg)
Source: NS&I, Morgan Stanley Research estimates

Exhibit 59: T-bill stock outstanding and the target for the end of this fiscal year
![](images/dd29f88f4cd89f3a0a4375d815a91474b851b1a62a8109ea559a379d7583524b.jpg)
Source: UK DMO, Morgan Stanley Research

On the other hand, the target for the end of the current fiscal year has been set at £65 billion (see Exhibit 59 ) as the DMO opted to meet the £10.5 billion reduction in the financing need for FY2023/24 mostly by decreasing T-bills sales by £10 billion. While among the reasons supporting this choice – the desire to keep the supply split as consistent as possible compared to the initial figures was pointed out by the DMO – we think that a reversal of the T-bills cut is likely for the upcoming fiscal year, in order to smooth the increase in gilt issuance.

## Further considerations on the bucket split

The shift in demand away from longs and especially ultras has already supported a shift in supply towards more mediums and shorts at the expense of longs and linkers over recent years – a trend that stands out from Exhibit 60 . The contribution of linkers has been reduced substantially from a previous norm of \~20% to \~11% for FY2023/24 while the contribution of longs has also reached historical lows, dragging the gross issuance weighted average maturity lower over the past couple of years (see Exhibit 61 ).

We expect supply to continue to be skewed towards more mediums and shorts in the coming years in line with the most recent trends. In linkers, we expect issuance to remain around the current contribution level going forward, potentially targeting a £25-30 billion range in cash terms. The other crucial aspect to consider is the type of longs and linkers being issued, which might skew the supply outlook in DV01 terms.

We expect the past year's trend to continue, with little appetite to issue ultra-longs and linker issuance to remain skewed towards 10-20-year paper and against ultras. This view is in line with the DMO's choice of not reopening the IL73 line over the past year while offering just \~£263 million of the 1E 73 via a tender in cash terms.

However, we doubt that little supply in ultras will support richer valuations as this reflects subdued demand. Furthermore, tender offers could become more frequent going forward, as a way to supply longs and ultras in limited size if demand arises.

An issuance skewed towards more shorts and mediums together with very little ultrabonds supply should limit the amount of supply in DV01 terms across lines. That said, the increase in financing needs should warrant higher supply or flat supply at best across buckets compared to the past year, which might provide little support for richer gilt valuations.

Exhibit 60: The bucket split is already trending towards less longs and linkers
![](images/0ef28b305ef2a912caaf1dec52769bb6d74cd4d75e43de17805ec1f434974452.jpg)
Source: UK DMO, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 61: The evolution of the weighted average maturity of gross issuance
![](images/6c06b860da827699675baa91979067e25df2b88328264628bc6b5c377a7ffc0a.jpg)
Source: UK DMO, Morgan Stanley Research

## Beyond the upcoming fiscal year

While current estimates remain uncertain, especially as elections have to be held by January 2025, we expect gilt supply to remain higher for longer on the back of higher funding needs (see Exhibit 62 ). This scenario is even more likely when accounting for QE and QT contributions. We expect the unwinding of the APF portfolio and active QT especially to increase the supply burden at least over the short term, and the amount of DV01 supplied to the market will probably increase despite the decrease in the weighted average duration of gross supply (see Exhibit 63 ).

In this section we estimate net supply (net of redemptions) for the upcoming five years and compared to history given that redemptions should support gilts demand and improve the supply/demand backdrop. However, two main considerations are crucial.

First, the impact of redemptions in DV01 terms is highly uncertain, given that no realistic assumptions can be made on how they will be reinvested.

Second, it is worth noting how redemptions are significantly lower once accounting for the BoE holdings. In Exhibit 62 we report historical and expected supply net of redemptions (ex BoE) and active QE and QT (red line). A clear trend appears, with supply expected at an all-time high for the upcoming fiscal year.

We also look at supply in DV01 terms (excluding redemptions for the above considerations) and we envision a similar trend as gross supply is expected to be at \~£275 million/bp for the upcoming year and to remain elevated over the forecasting period (see Exhibit 63 ).

While the supply picture poses a risk for gilt valuations, we remain skeptical that supply will be the main driver of the gilt market at least in the short term and especially for outright valuations as fundamentals should matter the most, with the evolution of Bank Rate and the inflation outlook among the most relevant factors. Having said that, ASW and cross-market gilt valuations might eventually cheapen structurally before they attract demand from overseas investors, who have been historically the major holders of gilts after insurance companies and pension funds.

Exhibit 62: Past supply and future estimates in cash terms
![](images/c2ba43e390111316d21023cc5277f818778dcbd11de143f5279419aaa5222ef3.jpg)
Source: UK DMO, Morgan Stanley Research estimates

Exhibit 63: Past supply and future estimates in DV01 terms
![](images/52051ba4907cbf731a9f74a9a060668bbbf17e5dac2b51fbb7eab504b68057ae.jpg)
Source: UK DMO, Morgan Stanley Research estimates

## Trade implications

Gilts valuations might be affected by the Budget announcement especially if this surprises market expectations, with the extent of the surprise mostly relevant for ASWs, crossmarket valuations and the shape of the yield curve – barring any tail risk event which could have long-lasting effect on gilt valuations outright.

That said, the market is likely expecting an increase in gilt supply for the upcoming year, and a remit in line with our forecasts should not impact valuations meaningfully. The bucket split will also be watched closely, and an increase in shorts and mediums at the expense of longs is likely expected and possibly already priced in, as indicated by the recent 30-year ASW outperformance on the 10s30s ASW box. Hence, we do not expect the initial reaction to be material if the bucket split change is in line with our estimates.

On the other hand, ASW valuations can be affected meaningfully should the shift from longs to shorts disappoint market expectations and especially if there is a higher-thanexpected gilt remit, which is the biggest tail risk event, in our view. Also the split in cash terms and the delta versus the past year eventually should matter and not the contribution in percentage terms: an higher-than-expected gilt remit could warrant an increase in supply across all the buckets compared to the past year, despite any bucket split change in percentage terms.

The type of longs that will be issued also matters for the evolution of the ASW complex, especially at the back end, which is less sensitive to repo considerations. Further 30-year ASW performance might require longs issuance to be more skewed towards the 20-year sector and at the expense of the 30-year sector – in discontinuity with the past year. With these considerations, we prefer to stay short outright ASWs ahead of the Budget and with a medium-term horizon.

On the curve valuations, we reckon that a shift towards more mediums and less longs could add flattening pressures. That said, this outcome should be widely expected and priced in and, unless the shift comes in significantly more pronounced than our expectations, we see a pretty muted reaction. As noted above, the main risk come from a bucket split that surprises market expectations in terms of more longs and less shorts in a higher-than-expected gilt remit – an event which is likely to drive the curve steeper, in our view. Meanwhile, the macro backdrop should continue to support more steepening ahead of the BoE easing cycle, hence we keep our structural steepening bias and recommend to fade any flattening in the aftermath of the Budget, which might provide an attractive entry point to for entering or re-engaging in steepeners, in our view.

• Trade idea: Maintain long UKT 1T37 versus short UKT 1T57 at 20.5bp, target 60bp, stop 0bp.

• Trade idea: Maintain short 4T38 ASW, entry -49.2bp, target -65bp.

• Trade idea: Maintain receive May'24 MPC at 5.04%, target 4.85%.

# Japan | Slower but higher?

MORGAN STANLEY MUFG SECURITIES CO., LTD.

Koichi Sugisaki Koichi.Sugisaki@morganstanleymufg.com

+81 3 6836-8428

## Markets had got ahead of themselves

The JGB market’s main focus this week was the February 8 speech by BoJ Deputy Governor Shinichi Uchida, the career central banker who is widely viewed as the key person behind monetary policy decisions.

Many had seemingly been hoping for some sort of signal about a possible exit from the negative interest rate policy (NIRP) in March or April and/or hints at a more hawkish trajectory for the short-term policy rate thereafter given that Governor Kazuo Ueda had at his January post-meeting press conference expressed optimism about a “virtuous cycle between wages and prices”.

The subsequently released “Summary of Opinions” had taken a similarly upbeat tone. But Uchida ultimately made no particular allusion to March or April policy normalization, limiting himself to a declaration that “the Bank will monitor developments in the virtuous cycle between wages and prices by carefully examining various data and information”.

He did, however, lay out the BoJ’s “basic thinking on potential revisions to individual measures” while effectively acknowledging that the “large-scale easing [that] has been in place for more than 10 years” might soon have outlived its purpose.

As we discussed in "Japan | Will Markets Price In An Aggressive BoJ Rate Hiking Cycle?", the timing of a NIRP exit will ultimately be less important to interest rate markets than the subsequent trajectory of the short-term policy rate. As explained by Uchida:

## BoJ governor Uchida's speech

The Bank's basic thinking is to examine the current situation of and outlook for economic activity and prices, and then set the policy interest rate at an appropriate level so that CPI inflation will be at around the 2 percent target.

He then added that while the actual path of the policy rate will “naturally” depend on “future economic and price developments”, as of now he found it “hard to imagine a path in which [the BoJ] would then keep raising the interest rate rapidly”. Moreover, “The Bank would, I think, maintain accommodative financial conditions”.

We also learnt from Uchida’s speech that the BoJ pays at least some attention to what has been priced into the market with regard to the policy rate when formulating its official forecasts for economic activity and prices.

Uchida acknowledged that “the market currently is assuming a very gradual path for the policy interest rate” (see Exhibit 64 ), but then added that “Nonetheless, the Bank does not project that inflation will significantly exceed 2 percent”.

The basic takeaway would appear to be that there is no reason to expect a faster (than “very gradual”) pace of BoJ rate hikes under current economic and inflation circumstances.

Uchida then took a gentle approach in addressing criticism that the BoJ might be underestimating the necessary policy rate level.

(…) some are of the opinion, especially among overseas market participants and economists, that in a state where inflation is 2 percent, the nominal interest rate that is neutral to the economy should be 2 percent even if the natural rate of interest is 0 percent in real terms. Or, based on this, some suggest that if the desired policy interest rate is calculated using a simple method like the Taylor rule, this would yield a much higher figure.

I will not try to argue here which view is correct, and would like to reiterate that it all depends on future economic and price developments. On this basis, let me suggest that it is a bit difficult to assess the situation in Japan through analogies with Europe and the United States.

He then stressed that a “difference with Europe and the United States, where medium- to long-term inflation expectations have been anchored at 2 percent, is that those expectations in Japan are still in the process of climbing toward 2 percent (see Exhibit 65 ). This means that an accommodative monetary policy is needed to lift inflation expectations further and to be mindful of the risk that they would fall again.”

Exhibit 64: BoJ market pricing
![](images/c724707332655ccf15cf7eac7a86b65bb553af84f94a17ab3b2eaef8e78e1e45.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg

![](images/2f4555f49995592f6b1308b03fa0f0723d7acb454e8556ceea7853da33a3ded2.jpg)

In last week’s report, we argued that it would be difficult for markets to price in a rapid pace of BoJ tightening given the continued lower underlying trend in latest Tokyo CPI print as well as recent movements in US interest rates.

Given Uchida’s subsequent remarks, we would now contend that a significantly more hawkish rate hike trajectory is unlikely to be priced in absent some major change in exogenous circumstances such as a renewed acceleration in inflation or another surge in the 10y UST yield (and higher USD/JPY).

On the topic of the “yield curve control” (YCC) framework, Uchida said that even if it were to be terminated, the BoJ “will take careful measures so as not to create discontinuity before and after the revision, and will make sure that the amount of JGB purchases will not change significantly and interest rates will not rise rapidly”.

As we discussed in "Japan | Lower BoJ Expectations Meet Positive Carry Trades", the BoJ’s purchases have already been tapered back to a level on a rough par with scheduled redemptions of its existing holdings and are thus having almost no impact on the monetary base.

Our economists expect that the current “inflation-overshooting commitment”— essentially a promise to keep expanding the monetary base until the “price stability target” is achieved—will be abandoned as part of policy adjustments at the March meeting.

However, they expect that the subsequent pace of balance sheet runoff will be quite gradual. With Uchida’s comments having backed up this view, we as yet find it difficult to envisage the term premium widening so significantly as to generate strong upward pressure on JGB yields.

## Suggested positioning

As discussed above, markets seemingly have little reason to price in a rapid BoJ rate hike trajectory given current economic and inflation circumstances. That said, some investors may still want to express views on BoJ “normalization” a little further out the curve in (say) the 5y–10y sector in anticipation for a "slower but longer scenario".

What about possible exogenous drivers? US inflation has continued to slow, with the annual CPI rebasing seemingly set to have much less of an impact than in 2023. Moreover, our US economists expect January retail sales data to show a slowdown due to seasonal adjustment effects. Signs that growth-related data may be weakening could cause markets to downwardly reprice the “neutral” level for the Fed’s policy rate.

With our US rates strategy team remaining bullish on duration and with little room for the market to price in a sharper rate hiking cycle within the short period of time, we maintain receiving 2y OIS position.

We meanwhile remain bearish on 30y JGBs. The February 7 auction did indeed go relatively smoothly, but we have yet to see any indication of life insurers (or others) becoming more aggressive in their purchases. We believe that domestic real money investors remain reluctant to make more sizable additions to their holdings until either after the BoJ “normalization” process kicks off or the Fed enters into a rate-cutting cycle.

Moreover, our updated estimates of rebalancing-related demand for January based on public pension fund asset allocations as of end-December are suggestive of bonds seeing only quite limited inflows (see Exhibit 67 ). We thus see scope for 30s to cheapen yet further on the curve (absent stronger demand from lifers or pensions) and accordingly suggest sticking with 30y ASW shorts.

Our new recommendation is to buy 20s via a 10s20s30s butterfly. The 20y sector is currently cheap to its fair value consistent with UST yield levels and the market-implied BoJ rate hike trajectory, which is likely a reflection of it lacking a fixed investor base that would be willing to buy even in a bear market (see Exhibit 67 ).

We thus believe that 20s might have the greatest potential to outperform if and when investor sentiment turns bullish once again, whereas the 30y sector could underperform owing to its unfavorable supply/demand balance and the 10y sector as yet appears to be overvalued and be vulnerable if the market prices in a "slower but higher scenario". The risk for this trade is that market sentiment continues to be bearish.
