## Launch of 10y Climate Transition JGBs

On February 14, Japan will launch the world’s first sovereign bond for the purpose of funding the transition to a decarbonized society. The government’s plan calls for issuance of around JPY20 trillion in so-called “Climate Transition” or “Green Transformation (GX) Economy Transition” bonds over the decade from FY2023, with the remainder of the current fiscal year to see roughly JPY800 billion offerings of 10y and 5y bonds on February 14 and February 27, respectively.

Other than for the fact that the issuance proceeds are to be earmarked for decarbonization-related R&D and investments, the GX JGBs will not differ in any substantive way from standard JGBs and are expected to receive identical treatment when it comes to BoJ operations and use as collateral.

But what differences might be observed in practice?

First, the GX JGBs are close to “green bonds” in as much as they will be issued under a framework stipulating mandatory reporting requirements and will receive a Second Party Opinion pertaining to alignment with various principles and guidelines. It thus seems possible that they could be priced at a “greenium” (green premium) owing to demand from ESG investors.

Second, the JPY800 billion /annual offerings will be quite small when compared to the regular monthly issuance amounts of JPY2.5 trillion and JPY2.7 trillion for standard 5y and 10y JGBs, respectively. The GX JGBs could thus end up attracting somewhat of a scarcity premium, particularly if they do turn out to be eligible for inclusion in the BoJ’s bondbuying operations.

Third, and perhaps most importantly, those purchasing GX JGBs will be deemed to be investing in “efforts on climate change” and, as such, eligible for participation in the BoJ’s “Funds-Supplying Operations to Support Financing for Climate Change Responses”, which like the more standard Loan Support Program offer financial institutions a means of obtaining zero-interest “back finance” from the central bank.

Participating financial institutions will also receive additional Macro Add-on Balance allowances (equivalent to twice the amount borrowed) allowing for greater arbitraging across the uncollateralized call market. Moreover, while loans will in principle have a one-year duration, in practice it will be possible to roll them over until end-March 2031.

A financial institution participating in the BoJ’s Climate Change operations will thus be able to earn the following yield by purchasing a GX JGB and lodging it at the central bank as collateral (ignoring hair-cut effect).

GX JGB yield – Climate Change operation interest rate + (Macro Add-on Balance interest rate – uncollateralized call rate/GC repo rate)

The bracketed term represents the additional return that can be generated via arbitrage (by borrowing funds in the uncollateralized call market/GC repo market and then parking them in the Macro Add-on Balance current account tier).

The interest rate applicable to Climate Change operations could end up being raised from zero in the event of the BoJ hiking its (short-term) policy rate (albeit with rolled-over loans perhaps remaining eligible for zero-interest treatment), but purchasing GX bonds still can enable financial institutions to earn the spread between the Macro Add-on Balance interest rate and the uncollateralized call rate/GC repo rate by way of an additional return.

The Climate Change operations are currently set to expire before 10y GX JGBs will mature, but arbitraging will be possible throughout the entire life of a 5y GX JGB (at least initially), for which reason we might very well end up seeing them priced at a premium.

We expect that GX JGBs will be in particular demand among regional financial institutions, which have recently tended to be the most active borrowers in the uncollateralized overnight call market (see Exhibit 68 ). Such demand for funds has matched up quite closely with increases in Macro Add-on Balance ceilings (other than those stemming from changes in the Benchmark Ratio) (see Exhibit 69 ).

It suggests that regional financial institutions have been using the BoJ’s Loan Support Program and Climate Change operations, etc, to gain additional Macro Add-on Balance allowances that can be used to earn 0% interest on funds borrowed at a lower rate in the uncollateralized overnight call market.

Exhibit 68: Net cash supply (-) / demand(+) in the uncollateralized overnight call market
![](images/b65051bcf40139d53b12c8b5a1d3e9d0d58af193c7d2dfeedc78be0ef8fe5691.jpg)
Trust banks ex investment trusts —Investment trusts —Regional Institutions—Life & Non-Life Insurance Companies —Foreign Banks—Financial Institutions for Agriculture & Forestries —City banks
Source: BoJ, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 69: Macro add-on balance on regional institutions vs net cash demand from the regional institutions
![](images/384e438a092b8c3c5f8fbe4ac020bf16c9f32942d29adb897a537b2853944c2a.jpg)
Source: BoJ, Morgan Stanley Research

Such behavior likely goes a long way towards explaining why the uncollateralized overnight call rate has been hovering so close to zero of late (see Exhibit 70 ).

Changes in balances for BoJ funds-supplying operations over the past few years have, in fact, been consistent with increases in the Macro Add-on Balances of regional financial institutions (see Exhibit 71 ), which suggests to us that they will indeed see an incentive to buy GX JGBs for the purpose of generating additional headroom that can be used to earn a profit on funds borrowed in the uncollateralized overnight call market.

For the three reasons stated above, we expect GX JGBs to trade at a premium to standard JGBs of the same maturity. Financial institutions touting their “decarbonization support” credentials may perhaps look to hold to maturity (or at least for the long term), thereby making supply/demand that much tighter.

And regional financial institutions look likely to be particularly eager buyers, especially of the 5y GX JGBs that will mature before the BoJ’s Climate Change operations are scheduled to be wound up. Quick bond market survey on January suggested that the median of market participants see roughly 1-2bp premium for 10y GX bonds vs JB373.

Source: BoJ, Morgan Stanley Research

## Currency & Foreign Exchange

## United States

USD | Pin the tail on the Dollar

Both USD/JPY and USD/EUR become notably more highly correlated to 10y yields on days when a 10y auction occurs than on the average day. The same is true for 30y auction days and 30y yields. These results suggest that 10y and 30y auctions matter for the USD in a way that other tenor auctions don't. We conclude that the USD is particularly sensitive to yield moves driven by 10y and 30y auctions.

## Europe

GBP | Is there a window for continued outperformance?

The March 6 Budget may generate additional GBP gains should it amplify GBP's already-high carry/vol ratio. A package that is both larger than expected but also viewed as sustainable could achieve this. Longer term, increased predictability in fiscal and trade policy, credibility in price stability and alacrity in potential GDP growth could see meaningful inflows to UK assets given their steep discount versus peers, bolstering GBP in the process.

## SEK | Higher rates SEK

With the Riksbank now on hold after a hiking cycle that brought the policy rate from 0% to 4%, the question pivots from "how high" to "how long". The answer to that question depends on how restrictive monetary policy currently is.

When looking at economic data, not only do we see that households on an aggregated basis pay twice as much in interest compared to before the hiking cycle, but also their net real wealth per capita has been decreasing. The combination of these two factors clearly is weighing on the economy as bankruptcy and composition hearings reach 21<sup>st</sup> century highs.

However, despite the effects of monetary policy being felt in the economy, if inflation doesn't play ball the Riksbank will not be able to cut rates. The longer the Riksbank maintains the current level of policy, the greater the probability that the pace of cuts increases. Hence, given the pass through from pace of cuts to currency, we maintain our short SEK position.

## Dollar Bloc

CAD/NOK | Pivoting from short GBP/NOK to short CAD/NOK

We pivot from short GBP/NOK positions to recommending short CAD/NOK positions with a target of 7.00 and stop of 8.25. Market pricing is consistent with a similar amount of easing from the BoC and Norges Bank, even though core CPI has not declined to same extent in Norway as in Canada. We expect CAD/NOK to decline from diverging policy expectations.

The annual CPI revisions confirmed the recent progress on US inflation, which kept the market-implied pace of Fed cuts consistent with a gradual and predictable cutting cycle. A continuation of such market pricing should support risk-sensitive currencies and weigh on CAD/NOK.

Finally, our oil strategists point to upside risks to Brent Crude prices from continued OPEC supply cuts. Given NOK's greater sensitivity to oil prices than

CAD, such an outcome would likely be negative for CAD/NOK.

## Japan

JPY | Waiting for lower US yields

While USD/JPY has approached 150.00 once again, we see limited risk that it will go higher from here, given that the Fed is now focused more on inflation than strong growth, making it difficult for US short-term r\* to be repriced higher on the back of any upside growth surprise. With EUR seemingly particularly vulnerable to rate cut speculation given the current economic and inflation backdrop, we maintain short EUR/JPY.

## USD | Pin the tail on the Dollar

LLC</td><td>Zoe Strauss</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Zoe.Strauss@morganstanley.com</td><td>+1 212 761-0407</td></tr></table>

Treasury auctions matter for the USD. But the effects are only detectable around 10y and 30y auctions.

The USD is highly correlated to Treasury yields. Exhibit 73 shows the correlation of daily changes in USD/EUR and USD/JPY to movements in different Treasury tenors from late 2015 to today. EUR is sensitive to a slightly shorter-tenor segment of the curve than JPY, but both are generally most highly correlated to the belly (3y - 7y) segment of the Treasury curve.

In recent months, the USD-Treasury correlation has appeared particularly strong on days when the Treasury auctions a new benchmark bond.

For example, the USD tracked long-end Treasury yields lower on January 11 as yields fell after a strong 30y auction. Similarly, the USD tracked long-end Treasury yields higher on November 9 as yields rose after a weak 30y auction.

Exhibit 73: The USD is most highly correlated to the Treasury curve belly
![](images/e6994fdfe7b6934c9571902ec8c096f0ed74708616dc0121a1fb6fa91a6f812b.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 74: The USD is generally positively correlated to the size of auction tails
![](images/2eff2fb2a46a51ecc36f90ffda550e4a24b4485480f735de7e76dc207b54db86.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 74 illustrates that the USD is positively correlated to the size of a Treasury auction tail (or through) – particularly in 5y, 7y, 10y, and 30y auctions.

The change in USD/JPY and USD/EUR is from 11am to 3pm on auction days. The correlation for most tenors is positive, meaning a tail is associated with a higher USD and a through is associated with USD lower.

The reason why tails are associated with USD strength is probably in large part because they are associated with rising yields as investors incorporate information from the auction result about market demand for duration.

However, another reason is likely because of increased market focus on the specific tenor being auctioned.

Exhibit 75 and Exhibit 76 show the USD/JPY and USD/EUR correlation to moves in Treasury yields.

The yellow bars show how changes in USD/JPY and USD/EUR between 11am and 3pm correlate to changes in each tenor of Treasury yields between 11am and 3pm every day.

The blue bars show what that correlation looks like only on days when an auction of that tenor occurs.

For tenors where the yellow bars and the blue bars are the same, these results suggest that the USD-Treasury correlation is unchanged on auction days of that tenor. For example, USD/JPY is equally correlated to 5y Treasury yields on days when there is a 5y auction as on days when there is no 5y auction. Whether or not a 5y auction occurs on a given day does not change USD/JPY's correlation to 5y Treasury yields on that day.

Exhibit 75: USD/JPY is particularly highly correlated to 10y and 30y yields on days when those tenors are auctioned...
![](images/8a369c266c4b7fbf84e8d06fa6dc562f4e1e5ed31e618df2b398de112a95a783.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 76: ...as is the EUR
![](images/67bd191faa4f66e6ff11847206281bcc97fb939030636ca32e1851fdb8a1a0ad.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

However, the charts indicate that both USD/JPY and USD/EUR become notably more highly correlated to 10y yields on days when a 10y auction occurs than on the average day. The same is true for 30y auction days and 30y yields.

These results suggest that 10y and 30y auctions matter for the USD in a way that other tenor auctions don't.

The elevated USD correlation to the 10y Treasury yield on days when 10y auctions are held (and same for 30y) aligns with our earlier finding that the USD is relatively highly correlated to the magnitude of 10y and 30y auction tails in Exhibit 74 .

Exhibit 77: Treasury auctions of other tenors...
![](images/80005bb8daf970ab7ddfde2fea95bf724622b4928668e985a1b0ab33da309e35.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 78: ...have little effect on correlations
![](images/6aeac695890471cda7aeb1156da54ec59503a83161e29f3d8f8540d4d362e734.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Finally, it might be the case that the elevated 10y and 30y correlations are not specific to days when 10y and 30y Treasury auctions (respectively) are held. Instead, it might be that any tenor Treasury auction leads to an elevated USD correlation to the 10y and/or 30y yield.

Exhibit 77 and Exhibit 78 suggest this dynamic does not hold. The blue and yellow bars show the same correlations as in Exhibit 75 and Exhibit 76 .

However, the green bars show the correlation of the USD to yields of that tenor on days when any Treasury auction is held - not only an auction for that tenor benchmark. The 10y and 30y correlations on all auction days are generally much closer to the baseline correlation every day.

In other words, the simple fact that the Treasury is holding a Treasury auction on a given day has little effect on the USD-10y yield correlation. However, 10y auctions themselves are associated with an elevated USD-10y correlation. And the same is true for 30y auctions and the USD-30y yield correlation.

In sum, this analysis suggests that the USD is particularly sensitive to yield moves driven by 10y and 30y auctions.

## GBP | Is there a window for continued outperformance?

MORGAN STANLEY & CO. INTERNATIONAL PLC

MORGAN STANLEY & CO. LLC

<table><tr><td>David S. Adams, CFA</td></tr><tr><td>David.S.Adams@morganstanley.com +44 20 7425-3518</td></tr><tr><td>Wanting Low</td></tr><tr><td>Wanting.Low@morganstanley.com +44 20 7425-6841</td></tr><tr><td>Dominic Krummenacher</td></tr><tr><td>Dominic.Krummenacher@morganstanley.com +44 20 7425-9781</td></tr><tr><td>Andrew Watrous</td></tr><tr><td>Andrew.Watrous@morganstanley.com +1 212 761-5287</td></tr><tr><td>Zoe Strauss</td></tr><tr><td>Zoe.Strauss@morganstanley.com +1 212 761-0407</td></tr></table>

When we talk to investors, there's a general sense of bearishness about the UK economy – low potential growth, elevated political uncertainty and poor risk-adjusted returns versus peers. This widespread reluctance has kept some UK assets trading at a steep discount to peers (see Exhibit 79 , Exhibit 80 and Exhibit 81 ).

GBP has bucked this trend and, compared to other assets, trades close to – in fact even a bit above – its fair value versus USD (see Exhibit 82 ). GBP's surprising resilience in the past year or so is chiefly explained by its high carry relative to volatility (see Exhibit 83 ) and a low bar from bearish expectations, rather than any real improvement in investors' optimism.

Exhibit 79: UK equities trade at a consistent discount to G10 peers...

![](images/c7edf5067561c9cff6624ff824607bc446bff79ca9e21285b9ac6a7972932a11.jpg)
Source: Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research
Exhibit 80: ...while fixed income has traded with an additional premium since the September 2022 'mini-Budget'

![](images/040de4a3f2c5aedc377f5ac2ea567b6853bb298b8970982337c2373260767486.jpg)

But is there a window opening up for investors to get more optimistic about the UK? That is, could GBP begin to gain not just because of its high carry, but also because of optimism about the growth outlook?

The answer could be yes – but a few necessary conditions mean that it's far from a certainty. But the upcoming March Budget could be the first step in a process that leads investors to become more optimistic about the UK.

In the near term, the 'best' budgetary outcome for GBP is one that enhances its carry/vol the most. As we observed in September 2022, size isn't everything, and a repeat of that scenario likely sees GBP fall amid elevated volatility.

But a Budget that is seen as supporting growth, reducing the BoE's ability to cut rates, while doing so in a sustainable and predictable way likely enhances the carry/vol GBP offers. In our currently directionless FX world, where carry trades remain an important explanation of FX positioning, high carry/vol is a critical catalyst for currency moves.

The market is already long GBP versus EUR at reasonably elevated levels (see Exhibit 84 ), suggesting that there is a bit of asymmetry here should the Budget underwhelm expectations. But we think that electoral incentives are such that a meaningful underspend is unlikely.

Exhibit 81: UK gilts carry a significant premium relative to neutral rates, reflecting high inflation but also the volatility risk premium
![](images/e5dd1b31973bcb270aa5303ee10824e326a854c1c9615f56cae60d7f43a7a039.jpg)
Source: National central banks, Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 82: But GBP is unique in that it is a UK asset which, we estimate, is modestly overvalued % Deviation from Fair Value (vs USD)
![](images/8ad49b3cc3caaf395f0bb38fa39d62023f07b421d60e16c9f5f181eb4c7e7233.jpg)

Over time, though, we think that there is a pathway for investors to get more bullish on GBP for 'good' reasons – not just carry, but growth. We think that the 'best case' scenario for GBP would come from:

• Fiscal policy: Fiscal policy needs to be expansionary enough to support growth, particularly with productivity-enhancing capital spending, but also on a sustainable and, importantly, predictable course.

• Monetary policy: Monetary policy that is hawkish enough to see a high degree of confidence in price stability, but dovish enough to avoid unnecessarily crimping growth for its own sake.

• Trade policy: Increasing signs of 'optimal' integration with Europe and predictability vis-à-vis future trading relationships to support long-term investment.

Why these outcomes? Because we think that the combination of sustainably higher growth, aided by sustainable and predictable policy contours, would reduce uncertainty (risk) and amplify returns.

Changes in fiscal policy can have different impacts on future growth. As noted above, tax cuts have a fiscal multiplier of 0.3. But fiscal spending in productivity-enhancing things like infrastructure can often have a higher multiplier, suggesting that wider deficits today could generate a faster pace of growth over time.

We think that investors would look quite favorably on productivity-enhancing capex as it would increase future returns on a sustainable basis.

Meanwhile, monetary policy could thread the needle here. Inflation has proven frustratingly sticky in the UK. While elevated Bank Rate has enhanced GBP's carry, it comes at an economic cost to debtors via higher mortgage rates and government interest expenses (particularly given that roughly a third of gilts are inflation-linked).

Exhibit 83: On average, GBP offers the highest carry/volatility across the G10

Exhibit 84: Speculators are short EUR/GBP because of its attractive carry versus volatility

![](images/c6e5dc28a8f1de4c819dbe7131b52077d568b5303924ef5644e1ff76cb057ab7.jpg)

Decelerating inflation would enable Bank Rate to fall back towards trend levels. This erodes GBP's carry, of course, but it also gives consumers and business scope to spend more and invest in new productive capacity. Increasing stability in inflation further reduces the inflation risk premia embedded in nominal interest rates.

Finally, the UK's trading arrangement with the rest of the world remains a key point of focus for investors. This is not to say that increased integration with the EU is automatically positive for the UK and GBP. But we think that investors have looked favorably on the increasingly positive relationship between UK and EU policymakers as a lack of acrimony enhances predictability. A shift towards modest integration could also be seen as enhancing UK potential growth given that the EU is such a large economic market, which investors would likely look favorably on too.

In sum, the upcoming Budget presents a potential GBP-positive catalyst if it enhances GBP's already-elevated carry/vol ratio. But fiscal, monetary and trade policy remain important potential catalysts to get investors more excited about the UK. Given that UK assets trade at a steep discount to peers, the scope for revaluations to drive capital inflows is substantial.

Pivoting short GBP/NOK to short CAD/NOK: With risks turning more balanced for GBP via fiscal policy, we pivot our long-term short GBP/NOK recommendation to short CAD/ NOK, as detailed in CAD/NOK | Pivoting from short GBP/NOK to short CAD/NOK .

## SEK | Higher rates SEK

<table><tr><td rowspan="7">MORGAN STANLEY &amp; CO. INTERNATIONAL PLC</td><td>Dominic Krummenacher</td></tr><tr><td>Dominic.Krummenacher@morganstanley.com +44 20 7425-9781</td></tr><tr><td>David S. Adams, CFA</td></tr><tr><td>David.S.Adams@morganstanley.com</td></tr><tr><td>+44 20 7425-3518 Wanting Low</td></tr><tr><td>Wanting.Low@morganstanley.com +44 20 7425-6841</td></tr><tr><td>Andrew Watrous</td></tr><tr><td>MORGAN STANLEY &amp; CO. LLC Andrew.Watrous@morganstanley.com +1 212 761-5287</td></tr><tr><td>Zoe Strauss</td></tr><tr><td>Zoe.Strauss@morganstanley.com +1 212 761-0407</td></tr></table>

Over the past two years the Riksbank has hiked its policy rate 8 times, from 0% to 4% ( Exhibit 85 ), in its quest to tackle inflation.

Now, the Riksbank has signalled (bar any surprising re-acceleration of inflation) that it is done with hikes. Hence, the focus has pivoted from "how high" to "how long".

Exhibit 85: The Riksbank has embarked on a rapid hiking cycle to tackle inflation
![](images/7cd43d84c0839052c301fc6854046404a7b321e3825a6223302a36a2916565bf.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

![](images/c6321eaa0db2ea504ab8008f50407350e5ae5d8e84af2fa48c8afc21e41776d5.jpg)

Despite the Riksbank embracing the possibility of a first cut in 1H24, Governor Thedéen still expressed his belief that "monetary policy needs to be contractionary for some time to ensure that inflation comes down and stabilizes at the target"

## So how long is "some time"?

That depends on how restrictive the current policy rate is, and the rate of pass through. Exhibit 86 seems to suggest that the pass through to households is quick, but not particularly restrictive.

The quick pass through is not particularly surprising given the distribution of loan tenors found in Swedish households ( Exhibit 87 ). More intriguing, however, is the transition from medium-term loans to short-term loans as the inflationary and hiking cycle began ( Exhibit 88 ).

Exhibit 87: Swedish households favor variable
![](images/f70cf1afdb51b1d5277ce3084cc8f41b062a4d06bfbf54b0dbf37f8e77274341.jpg)
Source: Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 88: Particularly since the Riksbank started hiking rates again
![](images/c823da77144874fa3a25765de3e2ca5a6fc847aa588a86775340e0874c53e24b.jpg)
Source: Mcrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

Given the development of the yield curve in Sweden ( Exhibit 89 ), households transitioned to front-end tenors as a result of the, originally, quicker increase in the longend of the curve.

The preference to keep mortgage rates floating over fixed prevailed, in the hope of either lower terminal rates than the market was pricing at the beginning or quicker rate cuts later on.

However, with front-end rates now higher than long-end rates, borrowers are stuck between a rock and a hard place. Pay more now, in the hopes that rates normalize lower, or lock in a high longer term rate and save upfront?

Either way, it is a lose-lose situation as the consumer has to pay more on a loan to finance real estate that has been decreasing in value from COVID period highs ( Exhibit 90 ).

But Exhibit 86 shows interest paid is still only 8% of disposable income — doesn't that mean there's still a large cushion to fall back on?

While it is true that 8% is only a modest number, it is important to consider where we came from. Prior to this hiking cycle, the same number was oscillating just below 4%.

Exhibit 89: Long-end yields rose first but now frontend yields are the highest and costing variable-rate borrowers
![](images/e9bbc21aa9cd95a9e9ebca9c48386a0df8c8bd6f3cb6af3e3b331196590d3d7e.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 90: At the same time real estate prices are coming down
![](images/7585ed88dd4e31f69ab7fcfd28e5236c41b89a3ab041603b8b85869d3a45cd01.jpg)
Source: Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

That means, assuming disposable income as a whole is unchanged, households are paying, on average, twice as much as originally budgeted. Taking into account the record high inflation and low negotiated wage growth in 2023/2024, the impact on disposable income is even larger.

When considering that these figures are aggregated across Swedish households, hence including both borrowers and non-borrowers, that suggests borrowers are in fact paying even more that twice what they had originally budgeted.

While this may not be surprising given Exhibit 89 , it is significant. Real estate is often a long-term investment and represents a significant portion of a household's assets and costs. Hence, a rapid increase in interest costs, is surely to weigh not only on households ( Exhibit 91 ), but across the economy.

Indeed, the proverbial belt-tightening effect can be seen in bankruptcy statistics ( Exhibit 92 ). Not only are the number of monthly bankruptcy and composition hearings rising rapidly, but they now stand at the highest levels since the turn of the century.

From this data, it seems undeniable that the tighter monetary conditions are inflicting pain on Swedish households and on the Swedish economy.

## If it's clear the Riksbank has to cut "soon" and market pricing agrees, why does it matter?

It matters because, as discussed in a prior note, the pace of cuts matters.

Should companies' pricing behaviour prove to be stronger than expected, the Riksbank may need to remain at terminal rate for longer, to have confirmation that inflation is improving as it would like.

Exhibit 91: Meanwhile the per capita real wealth has plummeted following the end of the post-COVID boom
![](images/da6d2f0cae294f8c0aeddd0c0f0f65643268b9e1df04ca40089ff1063417c114.jpg)
Source: Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 92: Bankruptcies and composition hearings (w/o bankruptcy) have been rising rapidly
![](images/4efe62e8159728b3360e14254665bd22246906a55e996c830713e3a716fd8d81.jpg)
Source: Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

In this case, our economists see an increasing risk to the size of cuts being larger (50bps) than our baseline (25bps). In turn, increasing the pace of cuts.

Hence, we recommend short SEK positions. In particular, we like pairing short SEK against long NOK positions to hedge for global macro risks, while profiting from what we expect to be significant growth divergence.

• Trade idea: Maintain long NOK/SEK at 0.9908 with a target of 1.06 and stop of 0.95

## CAD/NOK | Pivoting from short GBP/NOK to short CAD/NOK

<table><tr><td>MORGAN STANLEY &amp; CO. LLC</td><td>Zoe Strauss</td></tr><tr><td>Andrew Watrous</td><td>Zoe.Strauss@morganstanley.com +1 212 761-0407</td></tr><tr><td>Andrew.Watrous@morganstanley.com</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>+1 212 761-5287</td></tr><tr><td>MORGAN STANLEY &amp; CO. INTERNATIONAL PLC</td><td>David S. Adams, CFA</td></tr><tr><td>David.S.Adams@morganstanley.com</td><td>+44 20 7425-3518</td></tr><tr><td>Wanting.Low@morganstanley.com</td><td>Wanting Low</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>+44 207425-6841</td></tr><tr><td>Dominic Krummenacher</td><td></td></tr><tr><td></td><td>Dominic.Krummenacher@morganstanley.com +44 20 7425-9781</td></tr></table>

We close our short GBP/NOK recommendation as we think there may be some fundamental reasons for investors to turn more constructive on GBP (see GBP | Is there a window for continued outperformance? for more information). We shift to short CAD/ NOK positions instead with a target of 7.00 and stop of 8.25.

We expect CAD/NOK to decline due to diverging policy expectations. In addition to the local macro factors, the annual revisions to the seasonal adjustment factors confirmed the recent progress on US inflation, keeping the market-implied pace of Fed cuts consistent with a soft landing. Our new trade recommendation also provides a partial hedge against developments in the oil market.

Exhibit 93: 3-5 expected cuts from the Fed points to a scenario in which risk-sensitive currencies should perform well
![](images/208944305f617c4cffdc5d49d74e318e3c10d8d988d1a858476219fde0d76c2b.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 94: Core CPI remains more elevated in Norway than in Canada
![](images/5a5d8387d6d4b9dae3759c8ccf522d31187df02bbedbc4c8872bb09a8b1cb2f5.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

We recently discussed how the market-implied pace of rate cuts has been a key driver of broad USD strength or weakness. However, only looking at the expected amount of cuts from the Fed masks some of the local macro stories for individual G10 currencies.

Inflation has declined across G10 over the past year, but there are still some substantial differences in how far underlying inflation is keeping CPI remains above target. Core CPI in Norway has fallen from a peak of 7%YoY to 5.3%, whereas CPI ex energy and food in Canada has dipped below 3.5%YoY in December.

Despite these variation in inflation levels, market pricing is consistent with around the same amount of cuts for Norges Bank and the Bank of Canada in 2024, while also longterm yields have declined to similar levels in Norway and Canada.

Exhibit 95: Investors expect a similar amount of easing from the BoC and Norges Bank in 2024
![](images/0729f7a500672da61286d2cedff273048b90c20539eaa7792feaaf034497417a.jpg)
Source: MS STIR Desk, Morgan Stanley Research
Exhibit 96: Both short and long-term yields have declined to similar levels in Norway and Canada

![](images/e7c3162ac9250e6ed8953ad5e82397f073ca9741deb34d92f1c1f41f870f43b4.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

However, recent commentary from the BoC suggests the Governing Council is incrementally preparing the market for a rate cut. Shelter price inflation appears to be the last category where the Governing Council wants to see some additional evidence before it can lower its policy rate. And importantly, Governor Macklem stressed that some of the price pressures from shelter inflation may be beyond the control of the BoC, as they relate to structural imbalances in the Canadian housing market.

Exhibit 97: Shelter inflation is biggest contributor to above-target inflation in Canada
![](images/d6a19ec9694172d6766a0d945adad4a7fea8312543de5fc9b1bacbecfcf2f520.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research
Exhibit 98: The BoC's policy stance is significantly more restrictive than Norges' stance despite Canada experiencing lower inflation

![](images/f4cd7b23815b2ff769b80fe37ea6de1742b99f6171b6a661c76dde613750d6c7.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

Meanwhile, the current policy stance is significantly more restrictive in Canada than in Norway. This degree of restrictiveness, measured as the difference between the respective policy rates and a market-implied neutral rate, has increased for Canada over the past months, while it remained roughly the same in Norway.

Exhibit 99: CAD/NOK should fall as markets continue to point to a gradual and predictable pace of cuts - a scenario keeping risk demand supported

![](images/ba0caf569f03c14dfcc44a1caa3c64bdfb1c2fc2cb4b5302258a17483d92798f.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research
Exhibit 100:CAD has diverged in its sensitivity to Brent Crude prices relative to NOK

![](images/ee3e38001847eff41f7ace34b44a446f0ce7c42aa987a90ca1399bb1b11d6a4c.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Macrobond, Morgan Stanley Research

We expect that the above-mentioned local dynamics will weaken CAD/NOK, though global factors will matter as well. This week, FX markets paid close attention to the annual US CPI revisions, given the risks that it could have altered the expected Fed policy path.

Our economists note that the revisions will not change Fed's perception of the softening trend in core PCE inflation, while the pace of cuts priced by markets (MSP1UT Index) stayed around 4 after the release. A continuation of such market pricing should be supportive of risk-sensitive currencies and weigh on CAD/NOK.

Finally, our oil strategists forecast rather stable oil prices this year, with a gradual decline to \~\$75/bbl by the end of the year. However, they see upside risks to oil prices in the near term.

Following the announcement that the Saudi government no longer requires Aramco to raise its long-term capacity from 12 to 13 mb/d, our colleagues think that the likelihood has increased that Saudi Arabia will continue to support a further extension of current OPEC cuts (see Aramco No Longer Directed to Raise Capacity – Three Implications).

CAD has been relatively insensitive to developments in oil prices in recent months and diverged from the degree of co-movement that NOK exhibits with oil. A rise in oil prices should therefore benefit NOK more than CAD and weigh on CAD/NOK.

The main risk to our trade recommendation would be markets pricing out cuts, including in the US, from upside surprises in inflation or hesitancy from policy makers to signal willingness to lower their policy rates.

• Trade idea: Enter Short CAD/NOK at 7.8376 with a target of 7.00 and stop of 8.25

• Trade idea: Exit Short GBP/NOK

## Koichi Sugisaki

Koichi.Sugisaki@morganstanleymufg.com

+81 3 6836-8428

USD/JPY had trended gradually lower through the end of January, but rebounded from below 147 to above 148 after the strong US Employment Situation release on February 2 (including upward revisions for previous months) appeared to trigger an upward repricing of the market’s expectations for the Fed’s terminal policy rate level following the commencement of cuts (i.e., short-term r\* ) (see Exhibit 101 ).

And we have since seen a further climb towards 150 as the JPY has weakened across the board on comments, by BoJ Deputy Governor Shinichi Uchida in a February 8 speech, that appear to have been viewed in a generally dovish light (relative to market expectations).

US stocks have continued to hit new highs despite a recent rekindling of concerns over commercial real estate (CRE) loans, while USD/JPY implied volatility is now near its lowest level since 2022 when the Fed started its rate hiking cycle (see Exhibit 102 ).

![](images/d4ab4dda4049ae627d4e229b5bec7250271c55bdd8b532c8156075a7473faa76.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg
Exhibit 102:Medium-term expiry volatility in USD/JPY seems to price in gradual Fed normalization

![](images/1c983a6245dfb120014e513732adf923ea2e8ea720fab6989b6804dd0995dfd6.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg

Moreover, markets are as yet pricing in a more gradual Fed rate cut trajectory and a much higher short-term r\* than implied by the Fed’s own projections (median forecast of 2.875% in 2026 on December dot plot), suggesting that there is no real belief in a scenario that could see the Fed-BoJ policy rate differential narrow much more than modestly.

This should of course be encouraging for JPY carry traders, and likely goes a long way towards explaining why USD/JPY remains richer than appears warranted by the Fed-BoJ policy rate differential alone (see Exhibit 103 ).

We do however find it difficult to envisage a significant further rally in USD/JPY from current levels. First, in contrast to the period from summer through autumn of last year, the Fed has now started to express more confidence in the possibility of milder inflation coexisting with strong growth, and of late appears to be focusing more attention on the former than the latter. The net upshot is that market pricing of short-term r\* has started to look less sensitive to upside surprises in growth data (see Exhibit 104 ).

Exhibit 103:USD/JPY has traded rich vs the fair value implied by US-JP policy differentials
![](images/a7544521bd444d7f30a0a843d08c11500be03234e86afad7196c7e54e5b901a0.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg

Exhibit 104:US short-term r\* pricing has started to become relatively insensitive to growth surprises
![](images/d7082d994930a630f0233e0bc2e0cb384dd3f90203d7f771664c185d219fc6eb.jpg)
Source: Morgan Stanley Research, Bloomberg

Second, on the Japan side, we see little reason why expectations should be dialed back without a marked change in the external environment, given that the BoJ has already hinted that the timing for normalization will be close. Scope for a further widening in the US-Japan policy rate gap would thus appear quite limited.

The euro meanwhile appears vulnerable to rate cut speculation given current economic and inflation circumstances. The ECB is still insisting that it will leave rates on hold for a period amid signs of strong wage growth and “sticky” services inflation, but the eurozone economy remains weak, so we see ample potential for a steeper rate cut trajectory to start being priced in at some point. We thus continue to recommend being short EUR/JPY.

## • Trade idea: Maintain short EUR/JPY at 161.10 with a target of 145 and a stop of 162

We stay neutral on the USD but see risks skewed to the upside. The USD remains bounded between the risk-positivity of expected rate cuts on the one hand and strong US growth and expectations for the US 2024 election on the other. Expectations for the US outlook have been rising, while the bar for a data surprise in Europe and China is decreasingly low, though there is also risk that continued pushback on 1H24 cuts from central bankers may push rates higher and equities lower. We think investors are viewing the USD as a potential US election hedge since it is more sensitive to changes in foreign and trade policy than other asset classes and these actions can be taken by the executive unilaterally regardless of unified or divided government.

Our view: Neutral

We remain bearish on EUR on crosses like versus JPY. Already-weak growth is meeting continued disinflation, and we think markets are underpricing the risk that weak Chinese growth and deflation will weigh on both growth and inflation in Europe further. We think EUR/USD deep outof-the-money puts are a potentially interesting hedge for US presidential election risks, particularly since we think the USD is sensitive to trade and foreign policies

We continue to favour NOK on a relative value basis. Norway CPI came in on Friday a touch stronger than consensus with 0.1%M in headline and 0.0%M in core. ## A good week for breakevens

Breakevens staged a solid performance in the very front end (see Exhibit 105 ), helped by a positive perception of data, and an uptick in oil prices related to the widening conflict in the Middle East. However, despite the uptick, breakevens outperformed their beta to oil prices (see Exhibit 106 ) – suggesting a strong performance overall. The CPI revisions didn't show a big change in the CPI trajectory – and breakeven initially widened, only for the gains to peter out over the day.

Exhibit 105:Moves in breakevens and real yields over the last week
![](images/77136ab7e62e5284baf5fdff5577396c4a968f052ad6ae5a7aa591dc602e45a8.jpg)
Exhibit 106:Moves in breakevens vs. moves implied by beta to oil prices

![](images/d6609e31eee04f01a832a2826bdff44833e6f713fe168e8eaa2f01208e65c2c3.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

One of the key reasons we have avoided breakevens over the last few weeks is that breakevens could face downside risks given the cooling inflation profile. On that note, the CPI print next week will be important. More specifically, the rent inflation print carries a high weight because a cooling of rent inflation – which is to be expected given the BLS's new tenant rent index (see Exhibit 107 ) – can reset the expectations of monthly prints lower – see Exhibit 108 . Our economists also expect deceleration from the goods side of CPI in the next print.

Exhibit 107:BLS's New Tenant Rent index vs. Zillow and CPI rent inflation
![](images/dc2cc09810d3296457d58401d9b902307c951f5d2429230cfac598703d5f379c.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 108:Monthly rent inflation path in the last 2 years
![](images/0caeee2f77536ef897da27b9428a59382543c191248af004055808d8eb151b11.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research
Overall, with cooling inflation, and hard landing risks not priced in at all, we do not see attractive risk/reward to be long breakevens even at currently optically low levels. We stay neutral on breakevens.

## Short-Duration Strategy

## United States

MMFs start the year strong: Year-to-date, money market funds (MMFs) have seen \$116bn of inflows. MMF AUM increased \$90bn in January, doubling the growth seen last year and coming in significantly above typical seasonal trends. In addition, seasonality could turn out to be another tailwind for further AUM growth in February. Besides seasonality, Fed cuts in the horizon should keep MMFs attractive relative to other cash alternatives such as T-bills and certificates of deposits.

WAM takes a pause: MMF weighted-average maturity (WAM) has recently stabilized around 38 days after more than doubling over the past year. This moderation is expected as MMFs have already repositioned their portfolio WAM back to historical levels. A stable WAM over the past days also reflects market expectations for cuts being pushed further out.

Holdings continue to shift to higher-yielding alternatives: MMF holdings for January continue to reflect MMFs deploying RRP cash into more attractive alternatives. UST debt and repo with dealers continue to be the main two destinations for MMFs. In addition, holdings of FHLB debt, CD/CP, and time deposits continue to see growth as bank demand for funding remains steady.

MMFs continue to have capacity to absorb more bills: Total MMF holdings of UST debt have gone back to early 2021 levels. At the same time, they still make up <40% of all assets. This compares with 50%+ in prior years and suggests that MMFs have more room to absorb this year's bills supply (we expect \~\$500bn for CY2024). This is also supported by a still-ample balance in the RRP.

## MMF AUM grows by \$116bn YTD

Year-to-date, money market funds (MMFs) have seen \$116bn of inflows. This keeps MMF AUM on track to continue making new record highs as front-end yields remain attractive ( Exhibit 109 ). As shown in Exhibit 110 , growth continues to be led by retail funds (\$64bn) while institutional funds have contributed to 48% of all YTD inflows (\$55bn).

In particular, prime funds continue to see steady inflows (\$59bn YTD and \$231bn in 2023) due to higher yields despite their relatively lower degree of safety. Given that these funds are able to take credit risk, this backdrop should keep unsecured wholesale funding markets (e.g., commercial paper, certificate of deposits, time deposits) largely functional in the event that bank funding needs increase further in the coming weeks (see here and more below).

MMF AUM increased \$90bn in January, doubling the growth seen last year and coming in significantly above typical seasonal trends ( Exhibit 111 ). In addition, seasonality could turn out to be another tailwind for further AUM growth in February. Inflows in 2023 came in at \$63bn, and MMFs have seen \$36bn of inflows on average from 2015 to 2019 for the month of February.

Besides seasonality, Fed cuts on the horizon should keep MMFs attractive relative to other cash alternatives such as T-bills and certificates of deposits. As shown in Exhibit 112 , the increase in MMF since 4Q23 has coincided with a significant compression in the 3m bill - MMF 7-day yield spread.

Exhibit 111:Month-over-month MMF AUM change
![](images/2b2c6d2a76c4abc177a584ddbc599e4b77d4b7909bf4148c99658908c2c529da.jpg)
Source: Crane Data, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 112:Total MMF AUM and 3m bill vs. MMF 7- day yield
![](images/4e73bc5c36d30d33cfa48c8209b3f25c36cb1c9a868a7d0a13e27b86993919c1.jpg)
Source: Crane Data, Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

As discussed previously, we continue to expect further inflows this year as MMFs remain attractive for investors looking for a cash alternative with a high degree of safety that provides yields not seen in decades. This should keep an eventual peak in MMF AUM still some time away.

## MMF WAM takes a pause

Despite further growth in MMF AUM, MMF weighted-average maturity (WAM) recently stabilized around 38-days after a more than doubling over the past year ( Exhibit 113 ). This was reflected in the week-over-week change in WAM, which has averaged zero in February. This compares with an average of 0.82 days week-over-week growth during 4Q23 ( Exhibit 114 ).

Exhibit 113:MMF WAM
![](images/8fccf9cdfa17e83ace6adb1749f8058e5a113debd9bc8d889eac9fac25c31d38.jpg)
Source: Crane Data, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 114:MMF WAM week-over-week change
![](images/89fad62dc52b179b75b2faa78b90c5060b74e92bf71c15550a41c82b6803b903.jpg)
Source: Crane Data, Morgan Stanley Research

This moderation is expected as MMFs have already repositioned their portfolio WAM back to historical levels ( Exhibit 115 ). In particular, WAM is already above the typical \~35 days WAM seen during previous periods in which the Fed has been on hold. Also, historical data suggest that once the Fed starts cutting, further increases in WAM are likely to be more gradual relative to the increases observed over the past year.

A stable WAM over the past days also reflects market expectations for cuts being pushed further out. As shown in Exhibit 116 , the odds of a 25bp cut at the upcoming March FOMC meeting have decreased to 10%, and investors only expect 1.5 cuts by the June FOMC meeting vs. 3 at the start of the year.

Exhibit 115:MMF WAM and 7-day yields over the past decades
![](images/d9d711cf685a2020f76b067c95c033bb125aee26f2915904c6599b5de33577c2.jpg)
Source: Crane Data, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 116:Number of 25bp cuts for the next 3 FOMC meetings
![](images/df3a92942271fea54a9721ee5c3ba72c787298463ee4283cff317450d18cfa01.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

Hence, this shift in market pricing has likely motivated MMFs to be more patient in extending. This, in combination with steady inflows into MMFs, has also explained why the RRP has been largely unchanged over the past 2 weeks (2/9 vs. 1/26) at \$569bn.

Looking ahead, we continue to see higher bills supply, further RRP depletion as repo rates remain above the RRP and firm further on the back of more UST supply, and a TGA surge in April as conducive to tighter funding conditions. We therefore maintain short 2y SOFR swap spreads (see here for more).

In addition, we remain long duration and think the potential of weaker data ahead could also support higher front-end receiving, and thus contribute to tighter 2y swap spreads.

## MMF portfolio allocation continues to shift in January

As expected, MMF holdings for January continue to reflect MMFs deploying RRP cash into more attractive alternatives ( Exhibit 117 ). As we have discussed before, UST debt and repo with dealers continue to be the main two destinations for MMFs. In addition, holdings of FHLB debt, CD/CP, and time deposits continue to see growth as bank demand for funding remains steady ( Exhibit 118 ).

Starting with US Treasury debt holdings, total bills increased by \$143bn in January. At the same time, FRNs decreased by \$34bn, continuing the decline seen in 2023. After being an attractive investment for MMFs as the Fed was hiking, FRNs seem to have lost their appeal as cuts lurk in the horizon.

Total repo outside the RRP surpassed \$1.9tr at January month-end, with FICC-sponsored repo reaching \$475bn and all other repo \$1.426tr. ![](images/b4905507c62a9a8af531080da37448d902eb9196483db3f0e72b452b5e468b64.jpg)
Source: Crane Data, Morgan Stanley Research

Exhibit 120:Holdings of FHLB debt, CD, CP, and time deposits
![](images/6f0d8c687cef3bad735d907198ad89bdc1d689190a08dca5e4feca4b378a19c1.jpg)
Source: Crane Data, Morgan Stanley Research

FHLB debt saw a \$45bn increase in January, while holdings of certificates of deposit (CD), commercial paper (CP), and time deposits with individual banks saw an aggregate increase of \$98bn ( Exhibit 120 ). Although we continue to see no apparent signs of bank funding stress, steady growth in these categories keep bank liquidity needs in focus as banks continue to face deposit competition and higher funding costs.

## MMF continue to have capacity to absorb more bills

Total MMF holdings of UST debt have gone back to early 2021 levels as shown in Exhibit 121 . At the same time, they make up <40% of all assets. This compares with 50%+ in prior years and suggests that MMFs have more room to absorb this year's bills supply (we expect \~\$500bn for CY2024). This is also supported by a still-ample balance in the RRP.

Turning to bills purchases in January, we note that MMFs continued to add over \$60bn over long-term (120+ days) bills as they continue to protect yields from future cuts. At the same time, front-end bills (1m or less) saw the most growth as shown in Exhibit 122 . This was likely driven the fact that these tenors avoid the March FOMC while 2m-4m bills overlap with FOMC meetings.

Source: Crane Data, Morgan Stanley Research

When looking at maturity buckets, most bills holdings (1.468tr or 74%) continue to be under 3-months ( Exhibit 123 ). At the same time, the concentration to 2m bills remains low (23%) relative to prior months, while holdings of 6m+ bills (15%) are relatively higher when compared to June 2023 and December 2022 ( Exhibit 124 ).

## Interest Rate Derivatives

## United States

Fed speak outweighing banking concerns: Concerns around the health of the banking sector have led some to price cuts as soon as May. This week's Fed speak suggested policy rates would not be lowered quite so soon. Ultimately, Fed funds pricing distributions suggest Fed speak prevailed.

Long duration, rather than conditional bull steepeners: Forwards are pricing in flat swap curves for some time. History suggests curves will steepen much more than what is currently priced. Should investors play for the cutting cycle via conditional bull steepeners? We prefer long 5y UST, and explain why.

Implied/Realized 2s30s correlations too low?: Implied correlation on 2s30s curve vol is low compared to realized. The potential for an aggressive cutting cycles poses a challenge to playing for a normalization, however.

MORGAN STANLEY & CO. LLC

Francesco.Grechi@morganstanley.com

Implied volatilities on short-dated expiry swaptions decreased this week, while longer expiry points proved resilient (see Exhibit 125 ). Gamma vol was negatively correlated to moves in underlying forward swap rates, which increased on the week (see Exhibit 126 ). Note, these changes are relative to last Thursday.

Fed speak this week was atypically homogenous in tone. Fed officials reiterated the need for further confidence on lowering inflation, and suggested rate cuts would likely take place later in the year. Friday's CPI revisions were generally uneventful, as noted by our economists, though survey and labor data was robust. Further concerns surrounding banking sector stability emerged, and our banking analysts do not see systemic concerns.

The decrease in modal YE 2024 pricing did not lead to more cuts priced in 2025. Exhibit 128 shows SOFR markets anticipate fewer than four 25bp cuts in 2025, down from the past few weeks. Given our economists' base case of eight cuts in 2025, this suggests there is ample negative term premium yet to be priced for the upcoming cutting cycle. We thus maintain our bullish stance, via long 5y UST.

## Duration over (conditional) curve

Forwards are pricing in flat swap curves to last for some time (see Exhibit 130 ). History suggests curves will steepen much more than what is currently priced. Indeed, over cutting cycles since 1995, 1y-forward curves before the Fed starts cutting have always been flatter than ex-post realized curves 1y later (see Exhibit 131 ).

## So are conditional bull steepeners attractive? We do not think so, for a few reasons:

1. Entry points: Spot curves provide more attractive entry points for steepeners (see Exhibit 132 ). For example, 2s30s steepeners in delta space are currently at -70bp, whereas in 3m-forward vol space, they are -50bp

2. Roll-down: Vol for 6m and 1y expiries is higher than vol for 1m and 3m expiries, on short tails. For long tails, the surface is flat across expiries. Buying, e.g., 1y5y receivers vs. selling 1y30y receivers, rolls negatively (see Exhibit 133 )

3. Vol pricing: Short tail vol is richer than long tail vol, for gamma expiries (see Exhibit 133 ). Conditional bull steepeners thus entail buying rich vol, while selling cheap vol. Zero-cost structures require worsening entry points, which as previously mentioned are already worse than entry points in delta space

4. Duration, not curve: Ultimately, we prefer to express bullish rates views via outright duration views, rather than curve. This is a central thesis to our long 5y notes trade recommendation, rather than curve trades

Implied correlations point to a different picture for 2s30s vol. Recall that the volatility of the 2s30s spread depends on 2y swap vol, 30y swap vol, and a correlation term between moves in 2y and 30y swaps. The level of correlation required to make implied curve vol consistent with each tenor's swaption implied vol is referred to as implied correlation. All else equal, an increase (decrease) in implied correlation will result in lower (higher) 2s30s curve vol.

Currently, implied correlations on the 2s30s curve (across expiries) are priced around 60- 70% (see Exhibit 136 ). This is low compared to realized correlations on the underlying forward swaps, over the recent past. Indeed, Exhibit 137 , shows that the ratios of implied/ realized correlations are around 0.7-0.8x.

Exhibit 136:Implied 2s30s correlation

Source: S&P Global, Morgan Stanley Research

What does this mean? Simply put, a pickup in implied/realized correlation ratios toward 1x would push 2s30s curve vol lower – all else equal.

The "all else equal" is, admittedly, doing a lot of work. The onset of a non-insurance cutting could increase volatilities, as we noted here. Moreover, the potential for further banking stress would richen curve vol. Indeed, 2s30s curve vol picked up during March 2023 as (1) vanilla vols increased, and (2) the correlation between forward 2y and 30y swaps decreased.

We therefore do not see value in playing for a normalization in 2s30s implied/realized correlations.

## Pivot Points

Pivot points are charting levels used by day traders to determine market direction, support, and resistance levels. We calculate weekly pivot points using the previous week's open, high, low, and closing levels.

In The Tactical Bull Market Is Back, we discussed a simple methodology based on the Ichimoku Kinko charting technique for classifying market movements as bullish, bearish, or range bound. Then, we define whether the market movement is cyclical or secular in nature. A cyclical move is shorter term in nature, and a secular move is longer term in nature. For cyclical moves, we further divide them into tactical and strategic. We use daily data to inform tactical moves, and weekly data to inform strategic moves. We use monthly data to inform secular movements.

# G4 Smarter (beta) Trading Strategy

MORGAN STANLEY & CO. LLC

Matthew Hornbach, CMT Matthew.Hornbach@morganstanley.com

+1 212 761-1837

Enhancements to a G4 10y government bond futures momentum strategy have produced higher Sharpe ratios and stronger returns, relative to total return government bond indices for the G4, US, Germany, Japan, and the UK since 2000. See A "Smarter" (Beta) Way to Trade G4 10y Futures Duration? for more information on these strategies.

## Trading Strategy 1 – "Trade Longs/Fade Shorts"

When the 5-day moving average crosses above the 20-day moving average, buy the futures contract (long duration) and hold for a 25-business-day period. When the 5-day moving average crosses below the 20-day moving average, buy the futures contract and hold for a 25-business-day period. In short, this strategy buys futures when the Simple Moving Average Crossover (SMAX) generates both a long and a short signal, given the historical outperformance of long signals traded long and underperformance of short signals traded short. Given that the SMAX could generate both a long and a short signal within the predefined holding period, an investor may have a 200% long position since each of the two signals would be traded in separate portfolio sleeves.

## Trading Strategy 2 – Trade "Longs Only"

When the 5-day moving average crosses above the 20-day moving average, buy the futures contract (long duration) and hold for a 25-business-day period. When the 5-day moving average crosses below the 20-day moving average, do nothing. In short, an investor ONLY trades long signals initiated by the SMAX given their historical precedent to outperform

## Bond Market Indicators

Our BMI(10) models are bullish for Canada and neutral otherwise. Vol-adjusted carry is bearish for all regions, while momentum is neutral to bullish for all regions. Equity market signals are bearish for Japan only. Business cycle indicators are bearish for Germany, the U.K., and New Zealand. FX signals are bearish for the U.S., the U.K., and Australia.

Our BMI(2) models are bullish for Japan and New Zealand. Vol-adjusted carry is bullish for Japan only, and momentum is bullish for all regions. Equity market signals and business cycle indicators are the same as for our BMI(10) models. FX signals are bullish for Germany, Japan, and New Zealand.

Our iBMI models are bullish for HICPxt and neutral for all other regions. Oil signal turned bullish across all regions. Momentum signal grew more bearish for TIPS, grew less bearish for UKTi & HICPxT and grew less bullish for JGBi. Equities signal grew more bearish for TIPS, UKTi, & HICPxT and grew less bullish for JGBi. Value signal grew less bullish for TIPS, UKTi, & HICPxT and grew less bearish for JGBi.

## Swap Spread Indicators

Our SSI(2) model s imply that 2y spreads are roughly 29bp wide to fair value on a 6m rolling lookback. The 2std threshold is met. Our model-implied fair value can be found on Bloomberg using the ticker MSSIUS2 Index.

Our SSI(10) models imply that 10y spreads are 55.7bp tight on a 6m rolling lookback. The 2std trading threshold is met. Our model-implied fair value can be found on Bloomberg using the ticker MSSIUS10 Index.

Our SSI(30) models suggest that 30y spreads are 72.2bp tight to fair value on our 2y lookback window. The 0.5std threshold is met. Our model-implied fair value can be found on Bloomberg using the ticker MSSIUS30 Index.

Based on each of the SSI models, the 2s10s spread curve is \~84.7bp flat to fair value using a 6m lookback. The 10s30s spread curve is about \~49.3bp flat to fair value using our 2y lookback window.

Detail on the variable selection and model construction of these Swap Spread Indicators can be found in Modeling Swap Spreads. Within the piece, we discuss the various fundamental and flow-related drivers of 2y, 10y, and 30y spreads, and use these variables to construct multivariate regression models. We then develop and test trading strategies that employ these models. Updates to model-implied fair values, as well as backtesting of trading signals, can be found below.

## Government Bond Supply

In the US, new 3y, 10y and 30y were issued for a total of \$121bn. These will settle in the next week when \$57.8bn coupons and \$145bn redemptions will be paid. In the euro area, we estimate €31.5bn of issuance against €3.1bn coupons and €28.6bn redemptions. In the UK, UKTi 0.75% Gilt 2033 will be issued for £1.5bn and UKT 4.625% Gilt 2034 for £3.75bn. This is against no coupons and no redemptions coming into the market. In Japan, 10y Climate Transition JGB will be issued for ¥800bn and there will be an auction for enhanced liquidity for ¥500bn. This is against no coupons and no redemptions coming into the market. In Canada, 5y CAN Mar 2029 will be issued for \$5bn against no coupons and no redemptions coming into the market. In Australia, ACGB 1% Dec 2030 will be issued for \$800m against no coupons or redemptions. In addition, ACGBi 2% Aug 2035 will be issued for \$150mn. In New Zealand, NZGBs will be issued for an estimated \$0.5bn against no cash flow coming into the market. In China, there will be no issuance for both CGB and LGB, against CNY5.9bn CGB coupons and no redemptions coming into the market.

## In Case You Missed It

## UK Fiscal Policy: Delaying the Inevitable

## 8 Feb 2024

For March, we put fiscal space at £23 billion and assume \~£15 billion of easing/year, largely via (income) tax cuts. We assume tax cuts will be largely reversed post-November to fund the likely Spending Review increase in current expenditure. In the long run, the fiscal picture looks challenging if rates remain structurally higher.

## LatAm Macro Strategy: Can CLP's Structural Underperformance Be Stopped? 8 Feb 2024

The fast erosion in carry remains CLP's main headwind: Over the past few weeks, we have been asked about catalysts that could reverse some of CLP's more structural underperformance. Fundamentally, the currency's main issue right now is the extremely fast erosion in carry over recent months, both on an outright basis and relative to peers. This has turned CLP into a favorite funding currency for other longs in the region, such as MXN, BRL or COP, in addition to an attractive short versus USD outright.

## Global Macro Strategy: Global FX Positioning: From Neutral to Long USD After US Jobs Report

5 Feb 2024

In the week ending Friday, February 2, options pricing data indicate that investors added long USD (DXY) positions, and increased short AUD and EUR positions. In the futures market, most USD shorts were added against CAD, while investors bought USD against JPY and NZD in the week ending Tuesday, January 30.

## Global EM Strategist: No Sliding Out of DMs

## 5 Feb 2024

The US is still too strong for EM to handle, especially as the EM rate differential versus the US falls further. This is likely to keep EM assets on the back foot and lagging risk in general. We leave our USD longs in place, namely THB, PHP and CLP. Sovereign credit is all about alpha at this point.

## Global Macro Strategist: Don't Stop Receivin

3 Feb 2024

Hold on to that bullish feelin'. Some weeks'll win, some'll lose. Bond bears are born to sing the blues. 2024 likely won't be a repeat of 2023 for the global rates markets. Easing is on the way; the pace and eventual rationale matters more than the exact timing. Stay long duration; neutral on USD.

## Definition of terms

Buy/Long: The analyst expects the total or excess return (depending on the nature of the recommendation) of the instrument or issuer that is the subject of the investment recommendation to be positive over the relevant time period.

Sell/Short: The analyst expects the total or excess return (depending on the nature of the recommendation) of the instrument or issuer that is the subject of the investment recommendation to be negative over the relevant time period.

Selling protection or Buying Risk: The analyst expects that the price of protection against the event occurring will decrease over the relevant time period.

Buying protection or Selling Risk: The analyst expects the price of protection against the event occurring will increase over the relevant time period.

Pay: The analyst expects that over the specified time period the variable rate underlying the swap agreement that is the subject of the investment recommendation will increase.

Receive: The analyst expects that over the specified time period the variable rate underlying the swap agreement that is the subject of the investment recommendation will decrease.

Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for recommendations included in the Morgan Stanley Fixed Income Research reports is 1 - 3 months and the price of financial instruments mentioned in the recommendation is as at the date and time of publication of the recommendation.

When more than one issuer or instrument is included in a recommendation, analyst expects one part of the trade to outperform the other trade or combination of other trades included in the recommendation on a relative basis.

For important disclosures related to the proportion of all investment recommendations over the past 12 months that fit each of the categories defined above, and the proportion of issuers corresponding to each of those categories to which Morgan Stanley has supplied material services, please see the Morgan Stanley disclosure at https:// ny.matrix.ms.com/eqr/article/webapp/45053cc2-ab1b-11ee-95af-a1eee2204a2c

## STOCK RATINGS

Morgan Stanley uses a relative rating system using terms such as Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated or Underweight (see definitions below). Morgan Stanley does not assign ratings of Buy, Hold or Sell to the stocks we cover. Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated and Underweight are not the equivalent of buy, hold and sell. Investors should carefully read the definitions of al ratings used in Morgan Stanley Research. In addition, since Morgan Stanley Research contains more complete information concerning the analyst's views, investors should carefully read Morgan Stanley Research, in its entirety, and not infer the contents from the rating alone. In any case, ratings (or research) should not be used or relied upon as investment advice. An investor's decision to buy or sell a stock should depend on individual circumstances (such as the investor's existing holdings) and other considerations.

## Global Stock Ratings Distribution

(as of January 31, 2024)

The Stock Ratings described below apply to Morgan Stanley's Fundamental Equity Research and do not apply to Debt Research produced by the Firm.

For disclosure purposes only (in accordance with FINRA requirements), we include the category headings of Buy, Hold, and Sell alongside our ratings of Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated and Underweight. Morgan Stanley does not assign ratings of Buy, Hold or Sell to the stocks we cover. Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated and Underweight are not the equivalent of buy, hold, and sell but represent recommended relative weightings (see definitions below). To satisfy regulatory requirements, we correspond Overweight, our most positive stock rating, with a buy recommendation; we correspond Equal-weight and Not-Rated to hold and Underweight to sell recommendations, respectively.

## Analyst Stock Ratings

Overweight (O or Over) - The stock's total return is expected to exceed the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis over the next 12-18 months.

Equal-weight (E or Equal) - The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis over the next 12-18 months.

Not-Rated (NR) - Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.

Underweight (U or Under) - The stock's total return is expected to be below the total return of the relevant country MSCI Index or the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.

Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.

## Analyst Industry Views

Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.
